Representations of shifted super Yangians and finite W W -superalgebras of type A
Kang Lu, Yung-Ning Peng

Abstract
. In this article, we study the representation theory of shifted super Yangians and finite W W -superalgebras of type A. A criterion for the finite dimensionality of irreducible modules is obtained in the standard parity case. Furthermore, we provide an explicit Gelfand-Tsetlin character formula for Verma modules of finite W W -superalgebras. As an application, we show that the centers of the finite W W -superalgebras associated to any even nilpotent elements belonging to the same general linear Lie superalgebra are all isomorphic to the center of the universal enveloping superalgebra.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B37.

1.
Introduction

Finite
W
-algebras are certain associative algebras determined by nilpotent elements in complex semisimple Lie algebras. Geometrically, they can be realized as quantizations of the Slodowy slices to the corresponding nilpotent orbits.
The study of finite
W
-algebras originated from Kostant’s celebrated work
[
26
]
, in which the finite
W
-algebras associated to principal (also called regular) nilpotent orbits are proved to be isomorphic to the center of the universal enveloping algebras. The modern terminology and the name itself were later introduced by Premet
[
44
,
45
]
. For a comprehensive history and overview of applications, we refer to the survey articles by Losev
[
27
]
and Wang
[
49
]
.

Under mild conditions with specific technical requirements depending on the type, one can define finite
W
-superalgebras associated to nilpotent elements in both basic classical and strange Lie superalgebras.
In recent years, finite
W
-superalgebras and their representations have been extensively studied by mathematicians and physicists from different points of view and using various approaches
[
10
,
12
,
42
,
43
,
48
,
54
,
50
,
51
]
.

The algebraic structure of finite
W
-algebras is significantly more intricate than that of universal enveloping algebras.
A cornerstone is the realization of a finite
W
-algebra associated to an arbitrary nilpotent element in a general linear Lie algebra in terms of the
shifted Yangian
, as established by Brundan-Kleshchev
[
6
,
7
]
. Their work generalized earlier results
[
26
,
46
]
from principal or rectangular nilpotent elements to the arbitrary case.
By studying shifted Yangians, the representation theory of finite
W
-algebras is then systematically developed in
[
8
]
, leading to fundamental results such as highest weight theory, the classification of finite-dimensional simple modules, and a Gelfand-Tsetlin character formula for Verma modules.

The aforementioned isomorphism in
[
7
]
for other types of Lie algebras was still unavailable in the literature, except for some very special cases, until a recent work
[
30
]
, in which the isomorphism is established for the case of type BC (and conjecturally for D) Lie algebras.
On the other hand, the corresponding results are relatively complete for type A Lie superalgebras.
In
[
41
]
, an explicit presentation of a finite
W
-superalgebra associated to an arbitrary even nilpotent element in a type A Lie superalgebra was obtained, superizing the main results of
[
7
]
and generalizing special cases in
[
3
,
4
]
.
Hence, it is natural to seek further results on the representation theory of finite
W
-superalgebras.
The goal of this article is to study the representation theory of the shifted super Yangian of type A in the spirit of
[
8
]
. As a consequence, several corresponding results of the representation theory of finite
W
-superalgebras of type A are obtained, generalizing some earlier results in
[
4
,
5
]
to non-principal even nilpotent case.

To quickly summarize our results in this introduction, we introduce some terminology; precise definitions can be found in the context.
A pyramid is, roughly speaking, a skew Young diagram
\pi
in French style, but not allowing any box hanging in the air.
In the super-setting, each row in
\pi
, and hence each box in
\pi
, is assigned a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-parity. Let
m
and
n
denote the number of even and odd rows, respectively, so that the height of
\pi
is
m+n
.
A pyramid
\pi
records an even nilpotent element
e
and a good
\mathbb{Z}
-grading, and hence corresponds to a finite
W
-superalgebra which we denote by
W(\pi)
.
On the other hand, a pyramid
\pi
corresponds uniquely to a triple
(\sigma,\ell,\mathfrak{s})
, where
\sigma=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
is a shift matrix of size
m+n
with entries in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
\ell
is the size of the largest Jordan block of
e
, and
\mathfrak{s}
is the parity sequence recording the parity of each row in
\pi
from top to bottom. We call
\ell
the level of
\pi
. The matrix
\sigma
(and the sequence
\mathfrak{s}
) allows one to define the shifted super Yangian
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
, which is a subalgebra of the super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}:=\mathrm{Y}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
.
In terms of a Drinfeld type presentation,
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is an associative superalgebra generated by the following symbols

\displaystyle\big\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i

\displaystyle\leqslant m+n,r>0\big\}

\displaystyle\cup\,\big\{E_{j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle\mid 1\leqslant j<m+n,r>s_{j,j+1}\big\}

\displaystyle\cup\big\{F_{j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant j<m+n,r>s_{j+1,j}\big\},

subject to certain relations in Section
2
. When
\sigma=0
, or when the pyramid is of rectangular shape,
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is exactly the whole super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
.

Let
I_{\ell}
denote the two-sided ideal of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
generated by
\big\{D_{1}^{(r)}\mid r>\ell-s_{1,m+n}-s_{m+n,1}\big\}
. It is proved in
[
41
]
that the quotient
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma):={\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}/I_{\ell}
is isomorphic to
W(\pi)
as superalgebras. One should note that
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is not closed under the restriction of the coproduct of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
.
Our first result is a description of the behavior of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
under the coproduct.

Theorem A

(Theorem
3.13
, Corollary
3.15
)
.

The restriction of the coproduct

\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}

induces a homomorphism

\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}).

Moreover, this homomorphism factors through the corresponding quotients to yield a homomorphism of finite
W
-superalgebras

\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime}).

We roughly explain the notation in the theorem above.
Starting with the pyramid
\pi
, we cut it vertically along its highest column to obtain two smaller pyramids
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, where
\pi^{\prime}
is the left pyramid and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is the right pyramid. Let
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, and
\ell^{\prime\prime}
denote the levels of
\pi
,
\pi^{\prime}
, and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
Identify
W(\pi)\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
,
W(\pi^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})
, and
W(\pi^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
, where
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
are the corresponding shift matrices of the same height
m+n
by allowing empty rows. Since we cut
\pi
at its highest column, it turns out that
\sigma^{\prime}
is lower-triangular and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
is upper-triangular. As a result, we have
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and we may identify
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
as a subalgebra of both
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
. This homomorphism allows one to construct the tensor product of modules for shifted super Yangians and finite
W
-superalgebras, under a certain compatibility assumption related to parities.

Now we turn our attention to the representations. Using a PBW-type basis and triangular decomposition, one can define the highest weight theory for
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
and hence the notion of Verma modules. Here, the role of the Cartan subalgebra is replaced by the Gelfand-Tsetlin subalgebra generated by
\big\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,\,r\geqslant 1\,\big\}
.
This subalgebra is a maximal commutative (purely even) subalgebra that is contained in any shifted super Yangian
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
.
It is convenient to use generating series
D_{i}(u)=1+\sum_{r\geqslant 1}D_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r}\in\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)[\![u^{-1}]\!]
to describe the results.
As usual, a highest weight vector in a
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module means a vector which is annihilated by all
E_{j}^{(r)}
while each
D_{i}^{(r)}
acts on it as a scalar, say
\lambda_{i}^{(r)}
.
Thus, by collecting these scalars, one obtains an
(m+n)
-tuple of power series in
u^{-1}
which we call an
\bm{\ell}
-weight:

\bm{\lambda}=\big(\lambda_{i}(u)\big)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n},

where each
\lambda_{i}(u)=1+\sum_{r\geqslant 1}\lambda_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r}
is a formal series in
\mathbb{C}[\![u^{-1}]\!]
with constant term 1.
Let
\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
denote the set of
\bm{\ell}
-weights. Given an
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, let
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
denote the corresponding Verma module for
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
and let
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
denote its irreducible quotient.
Our next result is a finite-dimensional criterion of
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
in the standard parity case.

Theorem B

(Theorem
4.9
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be the standard parity sequence. For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, a finite dimensionality criterion for
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
in terms of Drinfeld polynomials is obtained.

We should mention that such a criterion for the whole super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
was only established for the standard parity sequence
[
53
]
. For an arbitrary parity sequence, an explicit criterion is still unavailable. However, using the notion of odd reflections for super Yangians
[
34
,
31
]
, one can still check the finite dimensionality of irreducible modules by recursively reducing them to the standard parity case.
Deducing an explicit criterion or a procedure checking the finite-dimensionality of
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
for an arbitrary parity sequence is an interesting yet non-trivial problem, which will be considered elsewhere.

Let
p_{i}
denote the number of boxes in the
i
-th row of
\pi
. If
v
is a highest weight vector in a
W(\pi)
-module, by a highly non-trivial technical result Theorem
3.3
one deduces that there exist complex numbers
\{a_{i,j}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}\}
such that

(u-\kappa_{i})^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})v=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j})v,\qquad\forall\,1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,

where
\kappa_{i}
’s are certain integers defined in (
2.44
).
Putting the scalars
a_{i,1},\ldots,a_{i,p_{i}}
into the boxes of the
i
-th row of
\pi
, we call the result a
\pi
-tableau. It turns out that the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weights for
W(\pi)
-modules are then parametrized by the set of row-symmetrized
\pi
-tableaux, which we denote by
\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
. For
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
, let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
denote the corresponding Verma module for
W(\pi)
defined in (
5.2
) and denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})
its irreducible quotient. Our next result is the classification of finite-dimensional irreducible
W(\pi)
-modules and a character formula for Verma modules.

Theorem C

(Theorem
5.4
, Theorem
5.6
)
.

We have the following.

(1)

When
\pi
is a standard pyramid (i.e. the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
corresponding to
\pi
is standard), a criterion for the irreducible
W(\pi)
-modules
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})
to be finite dimensional is obtained.

(2)

For
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
, an explicit character formula (
5.6
) for
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is obtained.

Since a
W(\pi)
-module is a
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module by pulling back of the map
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\cong W(\pi)
, Part (1) of Theorem
C
can be thought as a consequence of Theorem
B
. Alternatively, it also follows from the corresponding results
[
8
, Thm. 7.9]
for non-super case and the classification result for the principal case
[
4
, Thm. 7.2]
.

For Part (2), the primary differences from the non-super setting arise from the relations in Part (1) of Lemma
5.9
as follows:

•

Certain vectors constructed in constructed in
[
8
, Lem. 6.17]
are sensitive to the ordering of the product and may vanish in the super case, see Corollary
5.10
and also
[
34
, §4]
;

•

the second relation in Lemma
5.9
(1) after taking higher order derivatives contains many unwanted residue terms, which makes the detail more complicated.

To bypass these obstacles, we first consider the
generic
\pi
-tableau case
, which not only prevents such a vanishing issue but also simplifies some complicated calculations.
Then the general case follows from that since the set of generic
\pi
-tableaux is Zariski dense in the set of all
\pi
-tableaux.

Finally, as an application of Part (2) of Theorem
C
, we prove that the centers of finite
W
-superalgebras for any even nilpotent elements in the same general linear Lie superalgebra are all isomorphic, confirming a conjecture
[
51
, Conj. 4.12]
for the case of type A.

Theorem D

(Theorem
5.16
)
.

For any pyramid
\pi
corresponding to an even nilpotent element in
\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N}
, the center
Z(W(\pi))
of the corresponding
W
-superalgebra is isomorphic to
Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N}))
. In particular, it depends only on
M
and
N
, and is independent of the shape of
\pi
.

This article is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we set up the notation and recall some preliminary results about shifted super Yangians.
In Section
3
, we recall the definition of finite
W
-superalgebras and some known results, particularly their relationship to shifted super Yangians. After that, we construct parabolic inductions, as replacements for comultiplications, for finite
W
-superalgebras, and then lift them to the corresponding shifted super Yangians. This allows one to construct the tensor product of modules, under certain compatibility assumptions on parities.
In Section
4
, we define highest weight modules, Verma modules, category
\mathcal{O}
, and
q
-characters (or Gelfand-Tsetlin characters) for shifted super Yangians.
These notions are extended to finite
W
-superalgebras, and the corresponding results are developed in Section
5
. In particular, we give a
q
-character formula for
W
-superalgebras, which leads to an isomorphism between the center of a finite
W
-superalgebra and the center of its corresponding universal enveloping superalgebra.

YP is partially supported by the NSTC grants 111-2628-M-006-006-MY3 and 114-2115-M-006-006-MY2, and by the National Center for Theoretical Sciences, Taipei, Taiwan. YP would also like to thank Ngau Lam for informative discussions.

2.
Shifted super Yangians

Throughout the paper we work over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathbb{N}
be the set of natural numbers (non-negative integers). Denote
\geqslant
the partial order on
\mathbb{C}
defined by
x\geqslant y
if
x-y\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.1.
Shifted super Yangians

We recall the shifted super Yangians following
[
41
]
.

We first fix some notations that will be frequently used. A
parity sequence of type
(m|n)
is a sequence
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n}
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
1
appears exactly
m
times. We shall call
\mathfrak{s}
a parity sequence when the type is clear from the context. The parity sequence
(1,\dots,1,-1,\dots,-1)
is called
standard
. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
, define
|i|\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by the rule:
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=(-1)^{|i|}
.

A shift matrix is a matrix
\sigma=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
of natural integers such that

(2.1)

s_{i,j}+s_{j,k}=s_{i,k}

whenever
|i-j|+|j-k|=|i-k|
. In particular,
s_{i,i}=0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
. A shift matrix
\sigma
is completely determined by the upper and lower diagonal entries
s_{i,i+1}
and
s_{i+1,i}
for
1\leqslant i<m+n
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
shifted super Yangian

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
is the unital
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra generated by the elements

(2.2)

\displaystyle\big\{D_{i}^{(r)},D_{i}^{\prime(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\big\},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\big\{E_{j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant j<m+n,r>s_{j,j+1}\big\},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\big\{F_{j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant j<m+n,r>s_{j+1,j}\big\},

where
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
D_{i}^{\prime(r)}
are even elements while

(2.5)

|E_{j}^{(r)}|=|F_{j}^{(r)}|=|{j}|+|{j+1}|,

and subject to the following relations:

(2.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{r}D_{i}^{(r-t)}D_{j}^{\prime(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{r}D_{j}^{\prime(r-t)}D_{i}^{(t)}=\delta_{r0}\delta_{ij},\,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\big[D_{i}^{(r)},D_{j}^{(s)}\big]

\displaystyle=0,

(2.8)

\displaystyle[D_{i}^{(r)},E_{j}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{i}(\delta_{i,j}-\delta_{i,j+1})\sum_{t=0}^{r-1}D_{i}^{(t)}E_{j}^{(r+s-1-t)},

(2.9)

\displaystyle[D_{i}^{(r)},F_{j}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{i}(\delta_{i,j+1}-\delta_{i,j})\sum_{t=0}^{r-1}F_{j}^{(r+s-1-t)}D_{i}^{(t)},

(2.10)

\displaystyle[E_{i}^{(r)},F_{j}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=-\delta_{i,j}\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}\sum_{t=0}^{r+s-1}D_{i}^{\prime(r+s-1-t)}D_{i+1}^{(t)},

(2.11)

\displaystyle[E_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}\Big(\sum_{t=s_{i,i+1}+1}^{s-1}E_{i}^{(t)}E_{i}^{(r+s-1-t)}-\sum_{t=s_{i,i+1}+1}^{r-1}E_{i}^{(t)}E_{i}^{(r+s-1-t)}\Big),

(2.12)

\displaystyle[F_{i}^{(r)},F_{i}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}\Big(\sum_{t=s_{i+1,i}+1}^{r-1}F_{i}^{(r+s-1-t)}F_{i}^{(t)}-\sum_{t=s_{i+1,i}+1}^{s-1}F_{i}^{(r+s-1-t)}F_{i}^{(t)}\Big),

(2.13)

\displaystyle[E_{i}^{(r+1)},E_{i+1}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle-[E_{i}^{(r)},E_{i+1}^{(s+1)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}E_{i}^{(r)}E_{i+1}^{(s)}\,,

(2.14)

\displaystyle[F_{i+1}^{(s)},F_{i}^{(r+1)}]

\displaystyle-[F_{i+1}^{(s+1)},F_{i}^{(r)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}F_{i+1}^{(s)}F_{i}^{(r)}\,,

(2.15)

\displaystyle[E_{i}^{(r)},E_{j}^{(s)}]

\displaystyle=[F_{i}^{(r)},F_{j}^{(s)}]=0\qquad\qquad\text{\;\;if\;\; $|i-j|>1$},

(2.16)

\displaystyle\big[E_{i}^{(r)},[E_{i}^{(s)}

\displaystyle,E_{j}^{(t)}]\big]+\big[E_{i}^{(s)},[E_{i}^{(r)},E_{j}^{(t)}]\big]=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\,|i-j|=1,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\big[F_{i}^{(r)},[F_{i}^{(s)}

\displaystyle,F_{j}^{(t)}]\big]+\big[F_{i}^{(s)},[F_{i}^{(r)},F_{j}^{(t)}]\big]=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\,|i-j|=1,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\big[\,[E_{i}^{(r)},E_{i+1}^{(t)}]\,

\displaystyle,\,[E_{i+1}^{(t)},E_{i+2}^{(s)}]\,\big]=0\;\;\text{if\;\;}m+n\geqslant 4,\,|{i+1}|\neq|{i+2}|,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\big[\,[F_{i}^{(r)},F_{i+1}^{(t)}]\,

\displaystyle,\,[F_{i+1}^{(t)},F_{i+2}^{(s)}]\,\big]=0\;\;\text{if\;\;}m+n\geqslant 4,\,|{i+1}|\neq|{i+2}|,

for all indices
i,j,r,s,t
that make sense.

Remark 2.2
.

If
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}
, then we may interchange
r
and
s
in the left-hand side of (
2.11
) since
E_{i}^{(r)}
is odd. As a consequence, the right-hand side is zero. Similarly for (
2.12
).

Remark 2.3
.

The shifted super Yangians here are dominantly shifted and they are closely related to finite
W
-superalgebras
[
41
]
. It is possible to define shifted super Yangians for arbitrary shifts, cf.
[
2
,
14
]
. We shall only focus on the dominant shifted ones as one of our main goals is to study representations of finite
W
-superalgebras.

When
\sigma
is the zero matrix,
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}:=\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(0)
is the
super Yangian
introduced in
[
37
]
and the above presentation for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
was obtained by
[
20
]
in the case of standard
\mathfrak{s}
, where the general
\mathfrak{s}
case was obtained in
[
40
]
and
[
47
]
independently. In most cases we work with a fixed
\mathfrak{s}
and omit it in our notation. In case if different choices of
\mathfrak{s}
’s are involved we write
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma,\mathfrak{s})
to emphasize which parity sequence we are using.

The super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
was originally defined via an RTT type presentation in
[
37
]
as follows. The super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
is a unital
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra generated by
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(r)}
of parity
|i|+|j|
for
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n
and
r>0
, subject to the relation

(2.20)

(u-v)[\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u),\mathsf{T}_{k,l}(v)]=(-1)^{|i||j|+|j||k|+|i||k|}(\mathsf{T}_{k,j}(u)\mathsf{T}_{i,l}(v)-\mathsf{T}_{k,j}(v)\mathsf{T}_{i,l}(u)).

Here

\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u)=\delta_{i,j}+\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r}\in\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}[\![u^{-1}]\!].

There exists an anti-automorphism
\tau:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
defined by the following assignment:

(2.21)

\tau(\mathsf{T}_{ij}(u))=(-1)^{|j|(|i|+1)}\mathsf{T}_{ji}(u).

The following surjective homomorphism
\operatorname{ev}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
is called the
evaluation homomorphism
, which is defined by:

(2.22)

\operatorname{ev}(\mathsf{T}_{ij}(u))=\delta_{ij}+\mathfrak{s}_{i}e_{ij}u^{-1}.

One can introduce the Gaussian generators
\mathsf{D}_{i}^{(r)},\mathsf{E}_{i}^{(r)},\mathsf{F}_{i}^{(r)}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
r\geqslant 1
by applying the Gauss decomposition to
\mathsf{T}(u):=(\mathsf{T}_{ij}(u))_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
. Specifically, let

(2.23)

\mathsf{D}_{i}(u)=1+\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{D}_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r},\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,

\mathsf{E}_{i,j}(u)=\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{E}^{(r)}_{i,j}u^{-r},\quad\mathsf{F}_{j,i}(u)=\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{F}^{(r)}_{j,i}u^{-r},\quad 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,

which satisfy the relations

(2.24)

\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{\min(i,j)}\mathsf{F}_{i,k}(u)\mathsf{D}_{k}(u)\mathsf{E}_{k,j}(u)

with the convention that
\mathsf{E}_{i,i}(u)=\mathsf{F}_{i,i}(u)=1
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
. In particular, we set

\mathsf{E}_{i}(u)=\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{E}_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r}:=\mathsf{E}_{i,i+1}(u),\quad\mathsf{F}_{i}(u)=\sum_{r>0}\mathsf{F}_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r}:=\mathsf{F}_{i+1,i}(u)

for
1\leqslant i<m+n
.

It is known from
[
41
]
that
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
can be identified as a subalgebra of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
via the homomorphism induced by

D_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto\mathsf{D}_{i}^{(r)},\quad E_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{i}^{(r)},\quad F_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto\mathsf{F}_{i}^{(r)}

for admissible indices. However, in general the elements
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(r)}
,
\mathsf{E}_{i,j}^{(r)}
, and
\mathsf{F}_{i,j}^{(r)}
in
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
differ from the elements
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
,
E_{i,j}^{(r)}
, and
F_{i,j}^{(r)}
in
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
, respectively, defined below in §
2.2
. From now on, we shall not distinguish
D_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(r)},F_{i}^{(r)}
and
\mathsf{D}_{i}^{(r)},\mathsf{E}_{i}^{(r)},\mathsf{F}_{i}^{(r)}
, respectively.

2.2.
PBW basis

For
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n
and
r>s_{i,j}
(resp.
r>s_{j,i}
), define the elements
E_{i,j}^{(r)}
(resp.
F_{j,i}^{(r)}
) inductively by the rule:

(2.25)

\displaystyle E_{i,i+1}^{(r)}

\displaystyle:=E_{i}^{(r)},\qquad E_{i,j}^{(r)}:=\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}[E_{i,j-1}^{(r-s_{j-1,j})},E_{j-1}^{(s_{j-1,j}+1)}],

(2.26)

\displaystyle F_{i+1,i}^{(r)}

\displaystyle:=F_{i}^{(r)},\qquad F_{j,i}^{(r)}:=\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}[F_{j-1}^{(s_{j,j-1}+1)},F_{i,j-1}^{(r-s_{j,j-1})}].

Denote by
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}:={\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}(\sigma)
(it is known that
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}(\sigma)
is independent of
\sigma
) the subalgebra of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
generated by all the
D_{i}^{(r)}
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
r>0
,
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{+}(\sigma)}
the subalgebra of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
generated by all the
E_{i}^{(r)}
with
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
r>s_{i,i+1}
, and
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}(\sigma)}
the subalgebra of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
generated by all the
F_{i}^{(r)}
with
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
r>s_{i+1,i}
.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
41
, Coro. 5.9, 5.10]
)
.

The supermonomials in the elements

\displaystyle\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\},

\displaystyle\{E_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\},

\displaystyle\{F_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{j,i}\},

respectively, taken in any fixed order form a basis for
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}
,
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{+}(\sigma)}
,
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}(\sigma)}
, respectively. Moreover, the multiplicative map

(2.27)

{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}(\sigma)}\otimes{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}\otimes{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{+}(\sigma)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}

is an isomorphism of superspaces.

Define the canonical filtration of
\mathcal{F}_{0}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\subset\cdots
of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
by declaring that all
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
E_{i,j}^{(r)}
and
F_{j,i}^{(r)}
are of degree
r
. In other words,
\mathcal{F}_{d}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is the span of all supermonomials in these elements of total degree at most
d
. Then the associated graded superalgebra
{\mathrm{gr}}\,{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is free supercommutative on generators

(2.28)

\displaystyle\{{\mathrm{gr}}_{r}\,D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\{{\mathrm{gr}}_{r}\,E_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\{{\mathrm{gr}}_{r}\,F_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{j,i}\}.

Also define the
positive
and
negative Borel subalgebras

(2.31)

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\geqslant 0}(\sigma):={\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{+}(\sigma)},\qquad\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\leqslant 0}(\sigma):={\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}(\sigma)}{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}}.

Clearly, by the defining relations of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
, they are indeed subalgebras of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
.

It is also convenient to introduce a different set of generators
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
as follows. Let

E_{i,j}(u):=\sum_{r>s_{i,j}}E_{i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r},\quad F_{j,i}(u):=\sum_{r>s_{j,i}}F_{j,i}^{(r)}u^{-r}

for
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n
and set
E_{i,i}(u)=F_{i,i}(u)=1
,
D_{i}(u)=\mathsf{D}_{i}(u)
from (
2.23
) by convention. Then define

(2.32)

T_{i,j}(u)=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}T_{i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\min(i,j)}F_{i,k}(u)D_{k}(u)E_{k,j}(u).

Clearly,
T_{i,j}^{(0)}=\delta_{ij}
and
T_{i,j}^{(r)}=0
for
0<r\leqslant s_{i,j}
.

Note that if
\sigma
is the zero matrix, then
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
corresponds to
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(r)}
in the (non-shifted) super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The associated graded superalgebra
{\mathrm{gr}}\,{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is free supercommutative on generators

\{{\mathrm{gr}}_{r}\,T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\}.

Thus, the supermonomials in the elements

\{T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\}

taken in some fixed order form a basis for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Note that
T_{i,j}^{(r)}=0
if
0<r\leqslant s_{i,j}
. Clearly, the elements
D_{i}^{(r)}
(resp.
E_{i,j}^{(r)}
and
F_{j,i}^{(r)}
) can be written as
T_{i,i}^{(r)}
(resp.
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
and
T_{j,i}^{(r)}
) plus a linear combination of monomials of total degree
r
in the elements
\{T_{a,b}^{(s)}\mid 1\leqslant a,b\leqslant m+n,0<s<r\}
. Since
{\mathrm{gr}}\,{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is free supercommutative on generators (
2.28
)–(
2.30
), the elements
\{{\mathrm{gr}}_{r}\,T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\}
also freely generate
{\mathrm{gr}}\,{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
.
∎

2.3.
Properties of shifted Yangians

In this subsection, we summarize a few important properties of shifted super Yangians that will be used later.

2.3.1.
Some isomorphisms

Let
\sigma
be a shift matrix and fix
\mathfrak{s}
.
Note that the transpose
\sigma^{t}
is also a shift matrix.
On the other hand, suppose that

\vec{\sigma}=(\vec{s}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
is another shift matrix satisfying the condition

\vec{s}_{i,i+1}+\vec{s}_{i+1,i}=s_{i,i+1}+s_{i+1,i}

for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
.
Denote by
\vec{D}_{i}^{(r)},\vec{E}_{j}^{(r)}
and
\vec{F}_{j}^{(r)}
the generators of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\vec{\sigma})
to avoid confusion.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
41
, Props. 5.13, 5.14]

(1)

The restriction of (
2.21
) gives rise to an anti-isomorphism
\tau:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{t})
such that

(2.33)

\tau(D_{i}^{(r)})=D_{i}^{(r)},\,\,\,\tau(E_{j}^{(r)})=(-1)^{|j+1|(|j|+1)}F_{j}^{(r)},\,\,\,\tau(F_{j}^{(r)})=(-1)^{|j|(|j+1|+1)}E_{j}^{(r)}.

Moreover, for
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n
, we have

(2.34)

\tau(E_{i,j}^{(r)})=(-1)^{|j|(|i|+1)}F_{j,i}^{(r)},\quad\tau(F_{j,i}^{(r)})=(-1)^{|i|(|j|+1)}E_{i,j}^{(r)}.

(2)

The map
\iota:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}({\vec{\sigma}})
defined by

(2.35)

\iota(D_{i}^{(r)})=\vec{D}_{i}^{(r)},\quad\iota(E_{j}^{(r)})=\vec{E}_{j}^{(r-s_{j,j+1}+\vec{s}_{j,j+1})},\quad\iota(F_{j}^{(r)})=\vec{F}_{j}^{(r-s_{j+1,j}+\vec{s}_{j+1,j})},

is an isomorphism.

For any power series
f(u)\in 1+u^{-1}\mathbb{C}[\![u^{-1}]\!]
, there exists an automorphism of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
given by

(2.36)

\mu_{f}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n},\qquad\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u)\mapsto f(u)\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u).

It is well known that
\mu_{f}
fixes each
E_{i}^{(r)}
and
F_{i}^{(r)}
and sends
D_{i}(u)
to
f(u)D_{i}(u)
. Hence
\mu_{f}
restricts to an automorphism to
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
.

2.3.2.
\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}
-grading

Let
\mathfrak{c}
be the (commutative) Lie subalgebra of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
spanned by the elements
D_{i}^{(1)}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
. Let
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{m+n}
be the basis for
\mathfrak{c}^{*}
dual to the elements
\mathfrak{s}_{1}D_{1}^{(1)},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}D_{m+n}^{(1)}
. We call elements of
\mathfrak{c}^{*}

weights
and elements of

(2.37)

\mathbf{P}_{m|n}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m+n}\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{i}\subset\mathfrak{c}^{*}

integral weights
. The
root lattice
associated to the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule
\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}
of
\mathbf{P}_{m|n}
spanned by the simple roots
\alpha_{i}:=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i<m+n
. Define the
dominance order
of
\mathfrak{c}^{*}
by the rule:
\alpha\geqslant\beta
if
\alpha-\beta
is a sum of simple roots.

It is often helpful to consider
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
as a superalgebra graded by the root lattice
\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}
. Explicitly, by the defining relations of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
and the notation, we can define a
\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}
-grading

{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}}\big({\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\big)_{\alpha}

of the superalgebra
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
by declaring that the generators
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
E_{i}^{(r)}
, and
F_{i}^{(r)}
are of degree
0
,
\alpha_{i}
,
-\alpha_{i}
, respectively. In particular, it follows from (
2.32
) that
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
is of degree
\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}
.

2.3.3.
Coproduct

It is well known that the super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
is a Hopf superalgebra with counit
\varepsilon:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathbb{C}
and coproduct
\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
respectively defined by

(2.38)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u))

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m+n}\mathsf{T}_{i,k}(u)\otimes\mathsf{T}_{k,j}(u).

Define

(2.40)

H_{i}(u)=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}H_{i}^{(r)}u^{-r}:=\big(D_{i}(u)\big)^{-1}D_{i+1}(u)

for
1\leqslant i<m+n
. Clearly,
H_{i}^{(0)}=1
. Denote
\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}(\sigma)
(resp.
\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}(\sigma)
) the two-sided ideal of the Borel subalgebra
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\geqslant 0}(\sigma)
(resp.
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\leqslant 0}(\sigma)
) generated by
E_{i}^{(r)}
(resp.
F_{i}^{(r)}
) for
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
r>s_{i,i+1}
(resp.
r>s_{i+1,i}
). Again, we simply write them as
\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0},\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}
when the shift matrix
\sigma
is zero.

The following proposition for the coproduct is a refinement of
[
29
, Prop. 2.7]
for the standard parity sequence, see also
[
31
, Prop. 2.6]
and
[
21
, Lem. 2.5]
.

Proposition 2.7
.

The coproduct
\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
has the following properties:

(1)

\Delta(D_{i}^{(r)})\equiv\sum_{s=0}^{r}D_{i}^{(s)}\otimes D_{i}^{(r-s)}
modulo
\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}\otimes\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}
;

(2)

\Delta(E_{i}^{(r)})\equiv 1\otimes E_{i}^{(r)}+\sum_{s=1}^{r}E_{i}^{(s)}\otimes H_{i}^{(r-s)}
modulo
\big(\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}\big)^{2}\otimes\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}
;

(3)

\Delta(F_{i}^{(r)})\equiv F_{i}^{(r)}\otimes 1+\sum_{s=1}^{r}H_{i}^{(r-s)}\otimes F_{i}^{(s)}
modulo
\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}\otimes\big(\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}\big)^{2}
.

Proof.

We prove it using the same argument as in
[
29
, Prop. 2.7]
. Let

\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)}:=D_{i}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{2}(D_{i}^{(1)})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}D_{i}^{(1)},

then by (
2.8
), we have
[\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)},E_{i}^{(r)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i}E_{i}^{(r+1)}
. Note that
D_{i}^{(2)}=\mathsf{T}_{i,i}^{(2)}-\sum_{j<i}\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(1)}\mathsf{T}_{j,i}^{(1)}
and
D_{i}^{(1)}=\mathsf{T}_{i,i}^{(1)}
. A direct computation implies that

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)})\in\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)}+E_{i}^{(1)}\otimes F_{i}^{(1)}+\mathbb{C}^{\times}E_{i-1}^{(1)}\otimes F_{i-1}^{(1)}+\big(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{+}\big)^{2}\otimes\big(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}\big)^{2}.

Note that
\Delta(E_{i}^{(1)})=E_{i}^{(1)}\otimes 1+1\otimes E_{i}^{(1)}
. Using
[\mathbf{D}_{i}^{(2)},E_{i}^{(r)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i}E_{i}^{(r+1)}
,
[F_{i}^{(1)},E_{i}^{(s)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i}H_{i}^{(s)}
, and
[E_{i}^{(r)},\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{-}]\subset\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\leqslant 0}
, one shows inductively Part (2). Similarly, one proves Part (3).

We then prove Part (1). It is clearly for the case
i=1
. By (
2.40
), it suffices to prove the statement with
D
replaced by
H
. Applying
[\Delta(F_{i}^{(1)}),?]
to Part (2) and using
[F_{i}^{(1)},E_{i}^{(s)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{i}H_{i}^{(s)}
, we find that

\Delta(H_{i}^{(r)})-\sum_{s=0}^{r}H_{i}^{(s)}\otimes H_{i}^{(r)}\in\Big[F_{i}^{(1)},\big(\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}\big)^{2}\Big]\otimes\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}.

Similarly, applying
[\Delta(E_{i}^{(1)}),?]
to Part (3), we have

\Delta(H_{i}^{(r)})-\sum_{s=0}^{r}H_{i}^{(s)}\otimes H_{i}^{(r)}\in\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{>0}\otimes\Big[E_{i}^{(1)},\big(\mathrm{K}_{m|n}^{<0}\big)^{2}\Big].

Combining the two equations above completes the proof of Part (1).
∎

Recall that we can consider
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
as a subalgebra of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
via the canonical embedding
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
such that the generators
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
E_{i}^{(r)}
, and
F_{i}^{(r)}
of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
map to the elements of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
with the same name. Write
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
where
\sigma^{\prime}
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
) is an lower (resp. upper) triangular shift matrix. Embedding the shifted super Yangians
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
,
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
, and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
into
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
via the canonical way.

Lemma 2.8
.

The coproduct
\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
restricts to a superalgebra homomorphism

(2.41)

\Delta:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}).

Proof.

This is established in Theorem
3.13
.
∎

In particular, when
\sigma
is upper triangular, then the coproduct
\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
restricts to

(2.42)

\Delta:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}.

More generally, if
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
, then there exists a homomorphism

\Delta^{\iota}:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})

obtained by twisting
\Delta
using isomorphism
\iota
, see Remark
3.14
.

2.4.
Quantum Berezinian and center of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}

Recall from
[
17
]
that the
kk
-
quasideterminant
of a
k\times k
matrix
X
, denoted by
|X|_{k,k}
, is given by

(2.43)

|X|_{k,k}:=d-ca^{-1}b,

where

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}a&b\\
\hline\cr c&d\end{array}\right)

so that
a
is an invertible square matrix of size
k-1
and
d
is a scalar. Then it is well known that

D_{k}(u)=\big|\mathsf{T}_{[k]}(u)\big|_{k,k}\in{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}[\![u^{-1}]\!],

where
\mathsf{T}_{[k]}(u)=(\mathsf{T}_{i,j}(u))_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant k}
is the
k\times k
-submatrix of
\mathsf{T}(u)
on the upper left corner.

For a given parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
, define
\kappa_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
recursively by

(2.44)

\kappa_{1}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }\mathfrak{s}_{1}=1,\\
-1,&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathfrak{s}_{i}+\mathfrak{s}_{i-1}),\qquad 1<i\leqslant m+n.

It follows from
[
19
, Theorem 1]
and
[
9
, Theorem 4.5]
(see also
[
47
]
) that the
quantum Berezinian

\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u)
of the matrix
\mathsf{T}(u)
introduced in
[
37
]
can be expressed as

(2.45)

\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u)=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}\mathcal{C}_{m|n}^{(r)}u^{-r}=\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}D_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\in\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)[\![u^{-1}]\!].

It is a group like element, by which we mean that

(2.46)

\Delta(\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u))=\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u)\otimes\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u).

In the case of the standard parity sequence, (
2.46
) first appeared in
[
37
, (15)]
where a detail proof is given in
[
38
, §4]
. The general case follows from the fact that quantum Berezinian of
\mathsf{T}^{\mathfrak{s}}(u)
for various
\mathfrak{s}
are identified under suitable Hopf superalgebra isomorphisms between super Yangians associated with different parity sequences, see e.g.
[
9
, (4.14)]
.

Denote by
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma))
the center of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
.

Proposition 2.9
.

The elements
\mathcal{C}_{m|n}^{(r)}
for
r\geqslant 1
are algebraically free generators of the center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma))
.

Proof.

When
\sigma=0
and
\mathfrak{s}
is the standard one, it is well known
[
20
,
38
]
that the set
\{\mathcal{C}_{m|n}^{(r)}\,|\,r\geqslant 1\}
is algebraically independent and it generates
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n})
. For a general
\mathfrak{s}
, the proof in the standard case works analogously as in
[
47
, Thm. 2.43]
.

We claim that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma))
for any
\sigma
.
Note that
D_{i}^{(r)}
belongs to
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{0}\subseteq\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0
. Then (
2.45
) implies that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma))
.
The other inclusion can be established by passing to the associated graded superalgebra
{\mathrm{gr}}^{L}\,\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
with respect to the loop filtration, with the help of
[
41
, Thm. 4.4]
and
[
38
, Prop. 3.8]
, and modifying the argument in
[
33
, Thm. 1.7.5]
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{E}(u):=(\delta_{i,j}+\mathfrak{s}_{i}e_{i,j}u^{-1})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant m+n
, define the following formal series

(2.47)

\zeta_{k}(u):=\big|\mathcal{E}_{[k]}(u)\big|_{kk}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})[\![u^{-1}]\!],

where
\mathcal{E}_{[k]}(u)
is the
k\times k
-submatrix of
\mathcal{E}(u)
on the upper left corner.
Set

(2.48)

z_{m|n,\mathfrak{s}}(u)=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}z_{m|n,\mathfrak{s}}^{(r)}u^{-r}:=\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}(u-\kappa_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\zeta_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})[\![u^{-1}]\!].

We simply write
z_{m|n}(u)
or
z(u)
when there is no possible confusion about which
\mathfrak{s}
we are using.
Clearly,
z_{m|n}(u)
is obtained by applying the evaluation map (
2.22
) to
\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}(u-\kappa_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{m|n}(u)
.

The series appeared in
[
37
,
32
,
5
]
for the standard parity sequence. It was also observed in
[
5
, Rmk. 2.5]
that
z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}(u)
can be defined for a general
\mathfrak{s}
. It is well known that the coefficients of
z_{m|n}(u)
generate the center of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
; see Theorem
3.17
below.

3.
Finite
W
-superalgebras

In this section, we first recall the definition of finite
W
-superalgebras and their relations to shifted super Yangians following
[
41
]
.
Then we develop some properties about finite
W
-superalgebras that will be used to study their representations.
In particular, a homomorphism called
parabolic induction
is constructed in (
3.22
). We lift its domain to the shifted super Yangian in Theorem
3.13
.
These homomorphisms allow one to construct the tensor product of modules.

3.1.
Pyramid

We recall the notion of pyramid
[
13
,
22
]
following
[
8
,
41
]
.
A
pyramid

\pi
of
level

\ell
is a sequence
\pi=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell})
of integers such that

(3.1)

0<q_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant q_{k},\quad q_{k+1}\geqslant\cdots q_{\ell}>0.

for some fixed integer
0\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
. One can visualize the pyramid
\pi
as a diagram consisting of
q_{1}
boxes
stacked in the first column,
q_{2}
boxes stacked in the second column,
\ldots
,
q_{\ell}
boxes
stacked in the
\ell
-th column, where columns are numbered
1,2,\ldots,\ell
from left to right. We say that
the pyramid is
left-justified
if
q_{1}\geqslant q_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant q_{\ell}
and
right-justified
if
q_{1}\leqslant q_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant q_{\ell}
.

For our purpose, we shall consider the pyramid of at most
m+n
rows of boxes. Let
p_{i}
be the number of boxes on the
i
-th row. Then it defines the tuple
(p_{1},\dots,p_{m+n})
of row lengths with

0\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant p_{2}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant p_{m+n}=\ell.

Note that we allow empty rows.
Fix a parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{s}_{m+n})
of type
(m|n)
.
We say the
i
-th row of
\pi
is an even (resp. odd) row if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=1
(resp.
-1
). We call a box in
\pi
even (resp. odd) if it belongs to an even (resp. odd) row.
Let
M
and
N
be defined by

M:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n\\
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=1\end{subarray}}p_{i},\qquad N:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n\\
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=-1\end{subarray}}p_{i}.

Namely,
M
is the number of even boxes in
\pi
, while
N
is the number of odd boxes in
\pi
.
We put integers from
{\mathbb{I}}=\{1,2,\ldots,M+N\}
into boxes of
\pi
by the following rule: put
1,2,\ldots,M
into the even boxes of
\pi
down columns, from left to right, and put
M+1,\ldots,M+N
into the odd boxes of
\pi
in the same way. For any
i\in{\mathbb{I}}
, let col(
i
) and row(
i
) denote the column and row in which
i
appears, respectively.

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, denote
m_{i}
(resp.
n_{i}
) the number of even (resp. odd) boxes in the
i
-th column of
\pi
. Pick some
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
as in (
3.1
) such that
q_{k}=m_{k}+n_{k}
is maximal among all
q_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
. Define a shift matrix
\sigma=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
to the pyramid
\pi
by setting

(3.2)

s_{i,j}:=\begin{cases}\#\{c=1,\dots,k\mid i>m+n-q_{c}\geqslant j\},&\text{ if }i\geqslant j,\\
\#\{c=k+1,\dots,\ell\mid i\leqslant m+n-q_{c}<j\},&\text{ if }i\leqslant j.\end{cases}

Note that the pyramid
\pi
can be recovered if the shift matrix
\sigma
and the level
\ell
are known.
For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
, one simply puts
\ell-s_{m+n,i}-s_{i,m+n}
boxes on the
i
-th row of
\pi
with
s_{m+n,i}
columns indented from the left and
s_{i,m+n}
columns indented from the right.

3.2.
Finite
W
-superalgebras

We recall the definition of
W
-superalgebra in terms of a fixed pyramid
\pi
as above.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N}
be the general linear Lie superalgebra consisting of
(M+N)\times(M+N)
matrices equipped with the standard
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}
. Denote by
(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
the non-degenerate even supersymmetric
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant bilinear form defined by

(x,y):=\operatorname{str}(xy)

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
, where
xy
stands for the usual matrix product and
\operatorname{str}
means the supertrace.
Endow the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\oplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{j}
defined by declaring that
e_{ij}
is of degree
(\operatorname{col}(j)-\operatorname{col}(i))
for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant M+N
.
Define the following subalgebras according to the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading:

\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{g}_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{p}:=\oplus_{j\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{g}_{j},\qquad\mathfrak{m}:=\oplus_{j<0}\mathfrak{g}_{j}.

Let
e\in\mathfrak{p}
denote the nilpotent element in
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
defined by

(3.3)

e:=\sum_{i,j}e_{ij},

summing over all pairs

i

j

appearing in the pyramid
\pi
.
It is a fact
[
22
]
that the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\oplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{j}
defined in this fashion is a good
\mathbb{Z}
-grading for
e\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. Note that in the setup of
[
41
]
this grading is doubled.

Define
\chi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by

(3.4)

\chi(y):=(y,e),\qquad\forall\,y\in\mathfrak{g}.

The restriction of
\chi
on
\mathfrak{m}
induces a 1-dimensional
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{m})
-module. Let
I_{\chi}
be the left ideal of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
generated by

\{a-\chi(a)\,|\,a\in\mathfrak{m}\}.

As a consequence of the PBW theorem, we have
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})=I_{\chi}\oplus\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
together with the following identification

\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\chi}\cong\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})

induced by the natural projection
\operatorname{pr}_{\chi}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
. One then defines the following
\chi
-twisted action of
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
by

a\cdot y:=\operatorname{pr}_{\chi}([a,y])\qquad\forall\,a\in\mathfrak{m},y\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}).

The associated
finite W-superalgebra
, which we often omit the prefix “finite” from now on, is defined to be the space of
\mathfrak{m}
-invariants in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
under the
\chi
-twisted action. That is,

(3.5)

W(\pi):=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})^{\mathfrak{m}}=\{y\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})\,\,|\,\,[a,y]\in I_{\chi},\text{for all }a\in\mathfrak{m}\}.

We adapt the multiplication of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
as the multiplication of
W(\pi)
.

In the special case when
\pi
consists of a single column, the corresponding nilpotent element (
3.3
) is zero and therefore
\chi=0
,
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{m}=0
. Thus, the corresponding
W
-superalgebra is precisely the universal enveloping superalgebra
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

3.3.
Identification

Recall that, for each
1\leqslant j\leqslant\ell
,
m_{j}
and
n_{j}
denote the number of even and odd boxes in the
j
-th column of
\pi
, respectively.
Pick some
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
as described in (
3.1
) and set
\hbar=m_{k}-n_{k}
.
Let
(\check{q}_{1},\dots,\check{q}_{\ell})
denote the
super column heights
of
\pi
, where
\check{q}_{j}
is defined by

(3.6)

\check{q}_{j}:=m_{j}-n_{j}.

Consider the ordered index set
{\mathbb{I}}:=\{1<\ldots<M<\overline{1}<\ldots<\overline{N}\}
, where
\overline{i}=i+M
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
while
M
and
N
are the numbers of even and odd boxes of
\pi
, respectively. The
Kazhdan filtration
of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})

\cdots\subseteq\mathrm{F}_{d}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\mathrm{F}_{d+1}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\cdots

is defined by declaring

(3.7)

\deg(e_{i,j}):=\text{col}(j)-\text{col}(i)+1

for each
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}
, where
\mathrm{F}_{d}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
denotes the span of all supermonomials
e_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots e_{i_{s},j_{s}}
for
s\geqslant 0
with
\sum_{k=1}^{s}\deg(e_{i_{k},j_{k}})\leqslant d
.
The Kazhdan filtration descends to filtrations of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
and
W(\pi)
by setting
\mathrm{F}_{d}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}):=\operatorname{pr}_{\chi}(\mathrm{F}_{d}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))
and
\mathrm{F}_{d}W(\pi):=W(\pi)\cap\mathrm{F}_{d}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
, respectively.
Let
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
and
{\mathrm{gr}}\,W(\pi)
denote the associated graded superalgebras.

Define
\rho=(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{\ell})
, where
\rho_{r}
is given by

(3.8)

\rho_{r}:=\hbar-\check{q}_{r}-\check{q}_{r+1}-\cdots-\check{q}_{\ell}

for each
r=1,\dots,\ell
.
For all
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}
, define

(3.9)

\tilde{e}_{i,j}:=e_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}{(i)}}\rho_{\operatorname{col}(i)},

where
\operatorname{pa}{(i)}:=0
if
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and
\operatorname{pa}{(i)}:=1
otherwise.

By calculation one easily shows that

(3.10)

\begin{split}[\tilde{e}_{i,j},\tilde{e}_{h,k}]=&\,(\tilde{e}_{i,k}-\delta_{i,k}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}(i)}\rho_{\operatorname{col}(i)})\delta_{h,j}\\
&\,-(-1)^{(\operatorname{pa}(i)+\operatorname{pa}(j))(\operatorname{pa}(h)+\operatorname{pa}(k))}\delta_{i,k}(\tilde{e}_{h,j}-\delta_{h,j}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}(j)}\rho_{\operatorname{col}(j)}).\end{split}

The homomorphism
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{m})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
induced by the character
\chi
can be explicitly described as follows.
For any
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}
, we have

(3.11)

\chi(\tilde{e}_{i,j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}(i)}&\hbox{if $\operatorname{row}(i)=\operatorname{row}(j)$ and $\operatorname{col}(i)=\operatorname{col}(j)+1$;}\\[8.53581pt]
0&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Now we recall certain elements in the universal enveloping superalgebra
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
defined in
[
41
]
. Although we slightly modify the expressions from
[
41
]
, the same series (
3.13
) will be obtained.
For
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n
and integer
0\leqslant x\leqslant m+n
,
we set

\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}^{(0)}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\delta_{i,j}&\hbox{if $i\leqslant x$;}\\[8.53581pt]
\delta_{i,j}&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

and for
r\geqslant 1
we define

(3.12)

\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}^{(r)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{r}(-1)^{r-s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\\
j_{1},\dots,j_{s}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\#\{1\leqslant t\leqslant s-1\,|\,\text{row}(j_{t})\leqslant x\}+\operatorname{pa}(i_{1})+\cdots+\operatorname{pa}(i_{s})}\tilde{e}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{e}_{i_{s},j_{s}}

where the second sum is taken over all
i_{1},\dots,i_{s},j_{1},\dots,j_{s}\in{\mathbb{I}}

such that

(1)

\sum_{1\leqslant t\leqslant s}(\operatorname{col}(j_{t})-\operatorname{col}(i_{t}))=r-s
;

(2)

\operatorname{col}(i_{t})\leqslant\operatorname{col}(j_{t})
for each
t=1,\dots,s
;

(3)

if
\text{row}(j_{t})>x
, then

\operatorname{col}(j_{t})<\operatorname{col}(i_{t+1})
for each

t=1,\dots,s-1
;

(4)

if
\text{row}(j_{t})\leqslant x
, then
\operatorname{col}(j_{t})\geqslant\operatorname{col}(i_{t+1})

for each

t=1,\dots,s-1
;

(5)

\operatorname{row}(i_{1})=i
,
\operatorname{row}(j_{s})=j
;

(6)

\operatorname{row}(j_{t})=\operatorname{row}(i_{t+1})
for each
t=1,\dots,s-1
.

Due to conditions (1) and (2),
\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}^{(r)}
belongs to
\mathrm{F}_{r}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
.
We further define the following series for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n
:

(3.13)

\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}(u):=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}^{(r)}u^{-r}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})[\![u^{-1}]\!].

In particular, if either
p_{i}=0
or
p_{j}=0
, we set
\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}(u)=\mathsf{T}_{i,j;x}^{(0)}
.

We are now able to recall the following result relating the shifted super Yangians and finite
W
-superalgebras.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
41
, Coro. 9.3 & Thm. 10.1]
)
.

The elements

(3.14)

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{T}_{i,i;i-1}^{(r)}\,|\,1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,\,r>s_{i,i}\}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{T}_{i,i+1;i}^{(r)}\,|\,1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n-1,\,r>s_{i,i+1}\}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{T}_{i+1,i;i}^{(r)}\,|\,1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n-1,\,r>s_{i+1,i}\}

in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
defined by (
3.12
) are
\mathfrak{m}
-invariants and they generate the subalgebra
W(\pi)
.
Moreover, there exists a unique surjective homomorphism

(3.17)

\Omega:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\twoheadrightarrow W(\pi)

such that

(3.18)

\Omega(D_{i}^{(r)})=\mathsf{T}_{i,i;i-1}^{(r)},\quad\Omega(E_{i}^{(r)})=\mathsf{T}_{i,i+1;i}^{(r)},\quad\Omega(F_{i}^{(r)})=\mathsf{T}_{i+1,i;i}^{(r)},

where the kernel of
\Omega
is the two-sided ideal generated by
\{D_{1}^{(r)}\,|\,r>p_{1}\}
.

In view of Theorem
3.1
, we define the
truncated shifted super Yangian

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
to be the quotient of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
by the two-sided ideal generated by the elements
\{D_{1}^{(r)}\mid r>p_{1}\}
. As a consequence of Theorem
3.1
, we identify
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\cong W(\pi)
from now on.
In particular, under this identification, the elements
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
E_{i}^{(r)}
and
F_{i}^{(r)}
in the quotient
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
are identified with corresponding elements in
W(\pi)
by (
3.18
).
Although omitted in the notation, the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
in the definition of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
is obtained by reading the parity of each row of
\pi
, from top to bottom.

The following PBW theorem for truncated shifted super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
is known.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
41
, Coro. 8.2]
)
.

The supermonomials in the elements

\displaystyle\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\},

\displaystyle\{E_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,s_{i,j}<r\leqslant s_{i,j}+p_{i}\},

\displaystyle\{F_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,s_{j,i}<r\leqslant s_{j,i}+p_{i}\}

taken in any fixed order form a basis for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
.

The following theorem is a super generalization of
[
8
, Thm. 3.5]
, which turns out to be crucial later.

Theorem 3.3
.

The generators
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
are zero for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n
and
r>s_{i,j}+p_{\min(i,j)}
.

The proof of Theorem
3.3
is lengthy and technical, thus we postpone it to the Appendix
A
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The supermonomials in the elements

\{T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n,s_{i,j}<r\leqslant s_{i,j}+p_{\min(i,j)}\}

taken in some fixed order form a basis for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Follows from the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
2.5
using Theorem
3.3
.
∎

3.4.
Parabolic induction

The goal of this subsection is to construct certain homomorphisms, generalizing the notion of baby comultiplications defined in
[
41
, Thm 6.1]
. These homomorphisms serve as comultiplications for
W
-superalgebras and shifted super Yangians, allowing one to construct the tensor product of modules under mild assumptions on parity sequence. In particular, most results in this subsection are generalizations of corresponding results of
[
7
, §11]
to general linear Lie superalgebras.

Choose non-negative integers
\ell^{\prime}
and
\ell^{{\prime\prime}}
such that
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{{\prime\prime}}
.
The leftmost
\ell^{\prime}
columns of
\pi
form a pyramid, which we denote by
\pi^{\prime}
.
Similarly, let
\pi^{\prime\prime}
be the pyramid consisting of the rightmost
\ell^{\prime\prime}
columns of
\pi
.
In this situation, we write the decomposition as
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
. For example,

(3.19)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$\overline{2}$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$\overline{5}$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\quad\otimes\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern
