height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$\overline{2}$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$\overline{3}$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

Suppose that there are
M^{\prime}
even and
N^{\prime}
odd boxes in
\pi^{\prime}
, respectively.
Let
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be the subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{gl}_{M^{\prime}|N^{\prime}}

defined with respect to
\pi^{\prime}
, and let
W(\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})^{{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}}
be the
W
-superalgebra associated with
\pi^{\prime}
.
Similarly, suppose that there are
M^{\prime\prime}
even and
N^{\prime\prime}
odd boxes in
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
One defines the subalgebras
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}=\mathfrak{gl}_{M^{\prime\prime}|N^{\prime\prime}}

and
W(\pi^{\prime\prime})=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime})^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}
, where
M=M^{\prime}+M^{\prime\prime}
and
N=N^{\prime}+N^{\prime\prime}
.

For all
1\leqslant j\leqslant N^{\prime}
, let
\overline{j}=M^{\prime}+j
be a shorthand notation.
Following exactly the same rule (
3.9
), one defines elements
\tilde{e}_{ij}
in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})
for all
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}^{\prime}=\{1,\ldots,M^{\prime},\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{N^{\prime}}\}
according to
\pi^{\prime}
.
Similarly, elements
\tilde{e}_{ij}
in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime})
for all
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}^{\prime\prime}=\{1,\ldots,M^{\prime\prime},\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{N^{\prime\prime}}\}
are defined according to
\pi^{\prime\prime}
with similar shorthand notation.

Define a homomorphism
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime})
by

(3.20)

\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\tilde{e}_{i,j}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tilde{e}_{i,j}\otimes 1&\hbox{if $\operatorname{col}(j)\leqslant\ell^{\prime}$,}\\
0&\hbox{if $\operatorname{col}(i)\leqslant\ell^{\prime}$ and $\ell^{\prime}+1\leqslant\operatorname{col}(j)$,}\\
1\otimes\tilde{e}_{\eta(i),\eta(j)}&\hbox{if $\ell^{\prime}+1\leqslant\operatorname{col}(i)$,}\end{array}\right.

where

\eta(i):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}i-M^{\prime}&\hbox{if $\operatorname{pa}(i)=0$,}\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{i-N^{\prime}}&\hbox{if $\operatorname{pa}(i)=1$,}\end{array}\right.

for all
i,j\in{\mathbb{I}}
satisfying
\operatorname{col}(i)\leqslant\operatorname{col}(j)
.

By definition,
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
is a filtered map with respect to Kazhdan filtrations.
We spell the result of
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
when applied to the distinguished elements
\mathsf{T}_{i,j;0}^{(r)}
in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

For
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n
,
r>0
,

(3.21)

\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\mathsf{T}_{i,j;0}^{(r)})=\sum_{s=0}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{m+n}\mathsf{T}_{i,k;0}^{(s)}\otimes\mathsf{T}_{k,j;0}^{(r-s)}.

Proof.

It follows from the explicit formula of
\mathsf{T}_{i,j;0}^{(r)}
and (
3.20
).
∎

We now provide another point of view about how
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
is defined.
Consider the standard embedding of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
into
\mathfrak{g}
. It embeds
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}
into
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
into
\mathfrak{m}
. We may think the map
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
is induced by the natural projection
\mathfrak{p}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}
composed by a constant shift.
Define
\chi^{\prime}\oplus\chi^{\prime\prime}:\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by taking the supertrace form with
e^{\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}
, where
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
are nilpotent elements associated to
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
Then
\chi^{\prime}\oplus\chi^{\prime\prime}
coincides with the restriction of the character
\chi:\mathfrak{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined by taking the supertrace form with the nilpotent element
e\in\mathfrak{g}
associated to
\pi
. As a result,
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
sends
\chi
-twisted
\mathfrak{m}
-invariants in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
to
(\chi^{\prime}\oplus\chi^{\prime\prime})
-twisted
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime})
-invariants in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore, the restriction of
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
to
W(\pi)
defines a homomorphism

(3.22)

\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime}),

which is again a filtered map with respect to Kazhdan filtrations.

We call the map (
3.22
)
parabolic induction
, naming after similar homomorphisms defined for affine
W
-algebras
[
18
,
25
]
. In fact, it can be traced back to a functor defined by Losev
[
28
]
, relating the module categories for different finite
W
-algebras.
As we shall see in Lemma
3.7
, it is also a generalization of the baby comultiplications in
[
41
, (8.5), (8.6)]
.
The parabolic induction is coassociative, as indicated in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}
for some non-negative integers
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}
and let
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime\prime}
, where
\pi^{\prime}
means the pyramid consisting of the leftmost
\ell^{\prime}
columns of
\pi
,

\pi^{\prime\prime}
means the pyramid consisting of the middle
\ell^{\prime\prime}
columns of
\pi
,
and
\pi^{\prime\prime\prime}
means the pyramid consisting of the rightmost
\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}
columns of
\pi
.
Then the following diagram commutes.

\begin{CD}W(\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime\prime})&@>{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}}}>{}>&W(\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime\prime})\\
@V{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}}}V{}V&&@V{}V{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}\otimes 1}V\\
W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime\prime})&@>{1\otimes\Delta_{\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime\prime}}}>{}>&W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime\prime})\end{CD}

Proof.

It follows from (
3.20
).
∎

Let
(\sigma,\ell,\mathfrak{s})
denote the unique triple corresponding to
\pi
and keep in mind the isomorphism
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\cong W(\pi)
in Theorem
3.1
. Let
t={\rm min}\{q_{1},q_{\ell}\}
denote the height of the shortest column of
\pi
.
Recall the baby comultiplications
\Delta_{L}
and
\Delta_{R}
defined in
[
41
, (8.5), (8.6)]
.

Suppose first that
\Delta_{R}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell-1}(\dot{\sigma})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}})
is defined, which means that
q_{\ell}\leqslant q_{1}
and there are
m_{\ell}
even and
n_{\ell}
odd boxes in the rightmost column of
\pi
. In this case
\dot{\sigma}
is given by
[
41
, (6.1)]
. Consider the triple
(\dot{\sigma},\ell-1,\mathfrak{s})
. This triple corresponds to the pyramid
\pi^{\prime}
obtained by removing the rightmost column of
\pi
. After identifying
W(\pi^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell-1}(\dot{\sigma})
, we may describe the map as
\Delta_{R}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}})
, just like
\Delta_{\ell-1,1}
in (
3.22
).

Suppose instead that
\Delta_{L}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell-1}(\dot{\sigma})
is defined, which means that
q_{1}\leqslant q_{\ell}
and there are
m_{1}
even and
n_{1}
odd boxes in the leftmost column of
\pi
. In this case
\dot{\sigma}
is given by
[
41
, (6.2)]
. Then the triple
(\dot{\sigma},\ell-1,\mathfrak{s})
corresponds to the pyramid
\pi^{\prime\prime}
obtained by removing the leftmost column of
\pi
. By similar identifications, we have
\Delta_{L}:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
, just like
\Delta_{1,\ell-1}
in (
3.22
).
The next result shows that the baby comultiplications
[
41
, (8.5), (8.6)]
are special cases of the parabolic induction (
3.22
).

Lemma 3.7
.

Whenever the baby comultiplications are defined, we have
\Delta_{R}=\Delta_{\ell-1,1}
and
\Delta_{L}=\Delta_{1,\ell-1}
, respectively.

Proof.

The statement is trivial when
\ell=1
so we assume
\ell\geqslant 2
. Suppose first that
\Delta_{R}
is defined. Examining (
3.20
), one finds that the map
\Delta_{\ell-1,1}
coincides with the injective homomorphism
\psi_{R}:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{p}})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}})
defined in the proof of
[
41
, Thm. 10.1]
. Since the composition
\psi_{R}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{R}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow W(\pi)
is exactly the identification
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\cong W(\pi)
, we deduce that
\Delta_{\ell-1,1}=\Delta_{R}
. The other case where
\Delta_{L}
is defined is similar, using instead the injective homomorphism
\psi_{L}:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{p}})
defined in the proof of
[
41
, Thm. 10.1]
, where the composition
\psi_{L}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{L}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow W(\pi)
is our identification.
∎

Iterating the parabolic induction (
3.22
)
(\ell-1)
times, which can be done in any order with the help of Lemma
3.6
, we split the pyramid
\pi
into its individual columns and obtain the following homomorphism

(3.23)

\mu:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}}).

Recall that
\mathfrak{h}\cong\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}}
.
For each
1\leqslant r\leqslant\ell
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant q_{r}
, let
\pi(r,i)\in{\mathbb{I}}
denote the entry of the
i^{th}
box, counted from top to bottom, in the
r^{th}
column of
\pi
. Define
e_{i,j}^{[r]}:=e_{\pi(r,i),\pi(r,j)}
, for all
1\leqslant r\leqslant\ell
,
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant q_{r}
. Then
\{e_{i,j}^{[r]}\,|\,1\leqslant i,j\leqslant q_{r},1\leqslant r\leqslant\ell\}
forms a basis for
\mathfrak{h}
.
Under the identification
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})\cong\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}})
,
e_{i,j}^{[r]}
is identified with

1^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes e_{\eta(\pi(r,i)),\eta(\pi(r,j))}\otimes 1^{(\ell-r)},

where

\eta(\pi(r,i))=\begin{cases}\pi(r,i)-(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r-1}),&\text{ if }\mathfrak{s}_{i}=1,\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\pi(r,i)-(n_{1}+\cdots+n_{r-1})},&\text{ if }\mathfrak{s}_{i}=-1.\end{cases}

For example, take
\pi
as the one in the left of (
3.19
). Then
e_{2,3}^{[4]}=e_{\pi(4,2),\pi(4,3)}=e_{6,\overline{6}}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
, which is identified with

1^{\otimes 3}\otimes e_{2,\overline{1}}\otimes 1^{\otimes 2}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{0|1})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2|2})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2|2})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2|1})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{0|1}).

The map (
3.23
) is then identified with a homomorphism

(3.24)

\mu:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}),

which is a filtered map where
W(\pi)
is equipped with the Kazhdan filtration (
3.7
) and
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
is equipped with the standard filtration.
Let
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
denote the corresponding associated graded superalgebra.
Define following constant translation

(3.25)

\zeta:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}),\qquad e_{i,j}^{[r]}\mapsto e_{i,j}^{[r]}+\delta_{i,j}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}{(i)}}(\check{q}_{r+1}+\cdots+\check{q}_{\ell}).

Let
\xi:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
be the homomorphism induced by the natural projection
\mathfrak{p}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
. Comparing (
3.8
), (
3.9
), and (
3.24
) as iterations of (
3.20
), one observes that the restriction of the composition
\zeta\circ\xi
to
W(\pi)
is exactly
\mu
. This homomorphism (
3.24
) is called the
Miura transform
. In fact, not only
\mu
but also the parabolic induction
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
is injective, as stated in the following results, generalizing
[
7
, Thm. 11.4, Coro. 11.5]
.

Theorem 3.8
.

The map
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\mu:{\mathrm{gr}}\,W(\pi)\rightarrow{\mathrm{gr}}\,\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
is injective and hence so is the Miura transform
\mu:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
.

Proof.

As (
3.23
),
\mu
can be understood as a composition of
(\ell-1)
copies of
\Delta_{L}
or
\Delta_{R}
in any order which makes sense. Since both
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\Delta_{L}
and
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\Delta_{R}
are injective
[
41
, Thm. 8.2]
, the statement follows.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

The map
{\mathrm{gr}}\,\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:{\mathrm{gr}}\,W(\pi)\rightarrow{\mathrm{gr}}\,W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
is injective for any
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}
and hence so is the parabolic induction
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

\mu
can be understood as a composition of
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
followed by
(\ell-2)
more maps.
∎

Recall that if
\vec{\pi}
is a pyramid obtained by horizontally moving some rows of
\pi
, then there is a canonical isomorphism induced from (
2.35
),

(3.26)

\iota:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\vec{\pi}).

The parabolic induction is compatible with these isomorphisms, as stated in the following lemma, generalizing
[
7
, Lem. 11.6]
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose that
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
and
\vec{\pi}={\vec{\pi}}^{\prime}\otimes{\vec{\pi}}^{\prime\prime}
are pyramids such that

{\vec{\pi}}^{\prime}
and
{\vec{\pi}}^{\prime\prime}
have same row lengths as
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Then the following diagram commutes:

\begin{CD}W(\pi)&@>{\iota}>{}>&W(\vec{\pi})\\
@V{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}}V{}V&&@V{}V{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}}V\\
W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})&@>{\iota\otimes\iota}>{}>&W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime})\otimes W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime}),\end{CD}

where
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}
are levels of
\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, with
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
\ell
. There is nothing to prove if either
\ell^{\prime}=0
or
\ell^{\prime\prime}=0
, hence the statement holds trivially in the base case
\ell=1
. Assume from now on
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}
with
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}>0
.
In any case, at least one of
\Delta_{L}
and
\Delta_{R}
is defined. We explain the inductive step in the case where
\Delta_{R}
is defined, where in the other case the argument is almost identical.
Note that when
\Delta_{R}
is defined on
W(\pi)
, by our assumptions on the lengths of rows, we ensure that
\Delta_{R}
is defined on
W(\vec{\pi})
as well.

Consider first that
\ell^{\prime\prime}=1
. By Lemma
3.7
,
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}=\Delta_{R}
for both
W(\pi)
and
W(\vec{\pi})
. Then the commutativity of the diagram can be checked by the explicit formula of
\Delta_{R}
and
\iota
in
[
41
, Thm. 6.1 (1)]
and
[
41
, (5.23)]
when they evaluate on parabolic generators.

Suppose now
\ell^{\prime\prime}>1
. Let
\rho,\hat{\rho},\rho^{\prime}
and
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}
denote the pyramids obtained by removing the rightmost column (which is of height
q_{\ell}
in all cases) of
\pi,\vec{\pi},\pi^{\prime\prime}
and
\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Consider the following cube

where the maps on the front and back squares are defined from the parabolic inductions, and the remaining maps are induced by isomorphisms
\iota
.
The top and bottom squares are commutative by the special case
\ell^{\prime\prime}=1
considered in the previous paragraph.
The front and back squares are commutative by Lemma
3.6
.
The square in the right is commutative by the induction hypothesis. Since the horizontal maps are parabolic inductions, all of them are injective by Corollary
3.9
. This implies that the square on the left is commutative, finishing the inductive step.
∎

In the remaining part of this section, we will lift the parabolic induction from the truncated shifted super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
to
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
.
We first introduce the notion of column removal on pyramids.
Let
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\dot{\ell}}\leqslant\ell
be a subset of the columns of
\pi
.
Joining these columns together, we obtain a pyramid of column heights
(q_{i_{1}},\ldots,q_{i_{\dot{\ell}}})
, denoted by
\dot{\pi}
.
Let
\sigma=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
and
\dot{\sigma}=(\dot{s}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
denote the shift matrices corresponding to
\pi
and
\dot{\pi}
, respectively.
By construction, we have
\dot{s}_{i,j}\leqslant s_{i,j}
for all
i,j
. This implies that
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma})
when identifying them as subalgebras of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
. This canonical embedding
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma})
factors through the quotients since
p_{1}\geqslant\dot{p}_{1}
by construction, inducing the following homomorphism

(3.27)

{\dagger}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\dot{\ell}}(\dot{\sigma}).

Identifying
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)
with
W(\pi)
and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\dot{\ell}}(\dot{\sigma})
with
W(\dot{\pi})
, it defines a homomorphism

(3.28)

{\dagger}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\dot{\pi})

sending the generators
D_{i}^{(r)}
,
E_{i}^{(r)}
,
F_{i}^{(r)}
of
W(\pi)
to
\dot{D}_{i}^{(r)}
,
\dot{E}_{i}^{(r)}
,
\dot{F}_{i}^{(r)}
of
W(\dot{\pi})
, respectively.
Let
\mu:W(\pi)\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
and
\dot{\mu}:W(\dot{\pi})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}})
denote the Miura transforms in (
3.24
). There exists an obvious projection
\hat{\dagger}:\mathfrak{h}\twoheadrightarrow\dot{\mathfrak{h}}
defined by

\hat{\dagger}(e_{i,j}^{[r]}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e_{i,j}^{[s]}&\hbox{if $r=i_{s}$ for some $s=1,\ldots,\dot{\ell}$,}\\[5.69054pt]
0&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Lemma 3.11
.

With the above notation, the following diagram commutes

\begin{CD}W(\pi)&@>{\mu}>{}>&\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})\\
@V{{\dagger}}V{}V&&@V{}V{\hat{\dagger}}V\\
W(\dot{\pi})&@>{\dot{\mu}}>{}>&\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}})\end{CD}

Proof.

One checks explicitly from (
3.14
)–(
3.16
), (
3.18
) and definition
(
3.12
) that

\hat{\dagger}
maps the elements

\mu(D_{i}^{(r)})
,

\mu(E_{i}^{(r)})
and
\mu(F_{i}^{(r)})

to
\dot{\mu}(\dot{D}_{i}^{(r)})
,

\dot{\mu}(\dot{E}_{i}^{(r)})
and

\dot{\mu}(\dot{F}_{i}^{(r)})
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Suppose that
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
and
\dot{\pi}=\dot{\pi}^{\prime}\otimes\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
, where
\dot{\pi}^{\prime}
and
\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
are obtained by removing columns from
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Then we the following commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}W(\pi)&@>{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}}>{}>&W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})\\
@V{{\dagger}}V{}V&&@V{}V{{\dagger}\otimes{\dagger}}V\\
W(\dot{\pi})&@>{\Delta_{\dot{\ell}^{\prime},\dot{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}}>{}>&W(\dot{\pi}^{\prime})\otimes W(\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}),\end{CD}

where
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}
are levels of
\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}

and
\dot{\ell}^{\prime},\dot{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
are levels of
\dot{\pi}^{\prime},\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
3.11
and the injectivity of the Miura transforms, the statement follows from the following commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})&@>{\sim}>{}>&\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\\
@V{\hat{\dagger}}V{}V&&@V{}V{\hat{\dagger}\otimes\hat{\dagger}}V\\
\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}})&@>{\sim}>{}>&\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{U}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime\prime}),\end{CD}

where the horizontal maps are induced by the obvious isomorphisms
\mathfrak{h}\cong\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}
and
\dot{\mathfrak{h}}\cong\dot{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}\oplus\dot{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

The next is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\sigma
be a shift matrix and write
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}

where
\sigma^{\prime}
is strictly lower triangular and

\sigma^{\prime\prime}
is strictly upper triangular.
Identify
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma),\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
as subalgebras of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
. Then the restriction of the comultiplication

\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}

gives a homomorphism

\Delta:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}).

Moreover, for
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant s_{m+n,1},\ell^{\prime\prime}\geqslant s_{1,m+n}
and
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}
,
this map
\Delta
factors through the quotients to define a homomorphism

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})

which, after identifying

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma)\cong W(\pi)
,

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})\cong W(\pi^{\prime})
and

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\cong W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
,
is precisely the parabolic induction
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
defined in (
3.22
).

Proof.

Recall from
[
41
, Rmk. 8.5]
that
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma),\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
, and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
are identified with the inverse limits
{\displaystyle\lim_{\leftarrow}\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma),\lim_{\leftarrow}\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})}
, and
{\displaystyle\lim_{\leftarrow}\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})}
, respectively.
The commutative diagram in Corollary
3.12
ensures that the maps
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
are stable as
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty
, showing the existence of the induced homomorphism

\lim_{\leftarrow}\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}),

lifting from the maps
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
for all
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant s_{m+n,1},\ell^{\prime\prime}\geqslant s_{1,m+n}
.
It remains to show that
{\displaystyle\lim_{\leftarrow}\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}}
agrees with the restriction of
\Delta
on
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
.

Let
X
denote some generator
D_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(r)}
or
F_{i}^{(r)}
of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
. Its image
\Delta(X)
can be calculated as follows: first express
X
in terms of a linear combination of monomials in
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}^{(r)}
by the explicit formulas in
[
40
, Prop. 3.1]
.
Then by (
2.39
),
\Delta(X)
equals a linear combination of monomials in
\mathsf{T}_{h,k}^{(s)}
.
Finally we rewrite all
\mathsf{T}_{h,k}^{(s)}
appearing in the expression back in terms of the generators
D_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(r)}
and
F_{i}^{(r)}
.
On the other hand, exactly the same procedure can be applied to calculate
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(X)
where
X
runs over generators
D_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(r)}
or
F_{i}^{(r)}
in
W(\pi)
for each
\ell
, using (
3.18
) and (
3.21
).
The crucial point is that the formulas expressing
\mathsf{T}_{i,j;0}^{(r)}
in terms of
D_{i}^{(r)},E_{i}^{(r)}
and
F_{i}^{(r)}
in
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
is completely the same as the explicit formula in
[
40
, Prop. 3.1]
, for both of them are deduced from applying Gauss decomposition to an invertible matrix.
This shows that
{\displaystyle\lim_{\leftarrow}\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}=\Delta}
.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

In fact,
all
parabolic inductions
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
can be expressed in terms of the comultiplication
\Delta
on
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
as follows.
Let
\vec{\pi}^{\prime}
denote the right-justified pyramid with the same row lengths as
\pi^{\prime}
, and let
\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
denote the left-justified pyramid with the same row lengths as
\pi^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\vec{\pi}=\vec{\pi}^{\prime}\otimes\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
.
Note that the parabolic induction
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\pi)\rightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
can be recovered from
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\vec{\pi})\rightarrow W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime})\otimes W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime})
by Lemma
3.10
, using the isomorphisms
\iota
. Now
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}:W(\vec{\pi})\rightarrow W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime})\otimes W(\vec{\pi}^{\prime\prime})
is one of the comultiplications described by Theorem
3.13
.

Let
\pi
and
\dot{\pi}
be pyramids with exactly the same height and row lengths, in particular both of level
\ell
.
Let
\sigma
and
\dot{\sigma}
be their corresponding shift matrices, respectively. Pick positive integers
\ell^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}
so that
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}
and
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
.
By horizontally moving some rows of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, one obtains a right-justified pyramid
\dot{\pi}^{\prime}
and a left-justified pyramid
\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
so that
\dot{\pi}=\dot{\pi}^{\prime}\otimes\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime}
.
The pair
(\dot{\pi}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
determines a shift matrix
\dot{\sigma}^{\prime}
, and similarly
(\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})
determines
\dot{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
.
Then we have
\dot{\sigma}=\dot{\sigma}^{\prime}+\dot{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
, where
\dot{\sigma}^{\prime}
and
\dot{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
are lower and upper triangular, respectively.
As a consequence of (
3.26
), we have
W(\dot{\pi})\cong W(\pi)
and
W(\dot{\pi}^{\prime})\otimes W(\dot{\pi}^{\prime\prime})\cong W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
.
Therefore we obtain the following surjective homomorphisms

Y_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma})\twoheadrightarrow W(\pi),\quad Y_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma}^{\prime})\otimes Y_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma}^{\prime\prime})\twoheadrightarrow W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime}).

The following result follows from Theorem
3.13
and Remark
3.14
.

Corollary 3.15
.

The following diagram commutes:

\begin{CD}\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma})&@>{\Delta}>{}>&\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\dot{\sigma}^{\prime\prime})\\
@V{}V{}V&&@V{}V{}V\\
W(\pi)&@>{\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}}>{}>&W(\pi^{\prime})\otimes W(\pi^{\prime\prime}).\end{CD}

The comultiplication of a Hopf algebra allows one to construct the tensor product of modules. In our setup, the parabolic induction (
3.22
) plays a similar role with an assumption depending on their parity sequences, which is a new phenomenon in
W
-superalgebra.
Let
\pi^{\prime}
,
\pi^{\prime\prime}
be two pyramids with
W(\pi^{\prime}),W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
their associated
W
-superalgebras, respectively. With the isomorphism (
3.26
), we may assume that
\pi^{\prime}
is right-justified and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is left-justified.
We further assume that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
are
\mathfrak{s}
-compatible
, by which we mean that every box in each row of the joint pyramid
\pi:=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
has the same parity.
For example, the following two pyramids on the left are not
\mathfrak{s}
-compatible, but the two in the middle are.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=11.40001pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$-$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$-$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$-$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$-$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.40001pt{\vss\hbox to11.00008pt{\hss$-$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.40001pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\vrule height=11.40001pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-11.40001pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.00008pt\hrule width=11.79994pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

Let
M^{\prime}
be a
W(\pi^{\prime})
-module and let
M^{\prime\prime}
be a
W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
-module. With the assumption that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
are
\mathfrak{s}
-admissible, it makes sense to define
\pi:=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
. Then the outer tensor
M^{\prime}\boxtimes M^{\prime\prime}
can be equipped with a
W(\pi)
-module structure by (
3.22
).

Remark 3.16
.

Recall the observation in
[
39
]
(see also
[
47
]
for a proof) that the definition of the super Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
is independent of the choice of the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
, up to isomorphism. A similar result holds for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
as follows. Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{z})
be the minimal admissible shape of
\sigma
and let
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
denote the corresponding Young subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
. Consider the obvious action of
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
permuting the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
. Then we have

(3.29)

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma,\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma,\omega\mathfrak{s}),\quad\forall\,\omega\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu},

where the notation
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma,\mathfrak{s})
emphasizes the choice of the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
.
We first establish this fact for the truncation
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma,\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma,\omega\mathfrak{s})
.
Indeed, this can be easily deduced from the fact that permuting the sequence
\mathfrak{s}
is equivalent to permuting the rows of
\pi
, and any permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
does not change the corresponding nilpotent element (
3.3
). Since the definition of
W
-superalgebra depends only on the even nilpotent element
e

[
16
,
54
]
, we have
W(\pi)\cong W(\omega\pi)
, which further implies that

(3.30)

\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma,\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\ell}(\sigma,\omega\mathfrak{s}),\quad\forall\,\omega\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}.

Then a similar inverse limit argument as in Theorem
3.13
deduces (
3.29
).
As a consequence, when necessary, one may first permute rows of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, making them
\mathfrak{s}
-compatible, and then construct the outer tensor of their modules.

3.5.
Harish-Chandra homomorphisms

In this subsection, we consider the case when
\pi
is a single column; that is,
\pi
consists of
M
even boxes and
N
odd boxes that are stacked into a single column with respect to some order. Note that the reading of
\pi
from top to bottom gives a parity sequence
(\mathfrak{s}_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant M+N}
in which 1 appears exactly
M
times.
Since the nilpotent element corresponding to
\pi
is zero, as a special case of Theorem
3.1
, the associated
W
-superalgebra
W(\pi)
is identified as
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
by the evaluation map (
2.22
).
To be explicit,
D_{i}^{(1)}\in W(\pi)
is identified with
\mathfrak{s}_{i}e_{i,i}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant M+N
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{c}
is the span of
\{D_{i}^{(1)}\,|\,1\leqslant i\leqslant M+N\}
, where
\mathfrak{c}^{*}
is spanned by
\{\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,1\leqslant j\leqslant M+N\}
such that
\varepsilon_{j}
is dual to
\mathfrak{s}_{j}D_{j}^{(1)}
.

It is well known that the center of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
can be identified with
I(\mathfrak{c})
, which is a certain subalgebra of
S(\mathfrak{c})
that we will recall immediately, by the Harish-Chandra map.
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant M+N
, let
x_{i}:=e_{i,i}
if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=1
and
y_{i}:=-e_{i,i}
if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=-1
so that
S(\mathfrak{c})=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{M},y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}]
.
The Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N}
is
\mathfrak{S}_{M}\times\mathfrak{S}_{N}
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{M}
permutes
\{x_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant M}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
permutes
\{y_{j}\}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant N}
. Then we have

(3.31)

I(\mathfrak{c}):=\left\{f\in S(\mathfrak{c})^{\mathfrak{S}_{M}\times\mathfrak{S}_{N}}\>\bigg|\>\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{j}}\equiv 0\pmod{x_{i}-y_{j}}\\
\text{for any $1\leqslant i\leqslant M$, $1\leqslant j\leqslant N$}\end{array}\right\}.

Moreover, a generating set of
I(\mathfrak{c})
is given by the elementary supersymmetric polynomials

(3.32)

e_{r}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{M}/y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}):=\sum_{a+b=r}(-1)^{b}e_{a}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{M})h_{b}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})

for all
r\geqslant 1
, where
e_{a}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{M})
means the
a^{th}
elementary symmetric polynomial and
h_{b}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})
means the
b^{th}
complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials.

By a normal order
\lhd
on
{\mathbb{I}}=\{1,\ldots,M,\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{N}\}
we mean a total order on
{\mathbb{I}}
such that
1\lhd\cdots\lhd M
and
\overline{1}\lhd\cdots\lhd\overline{N}
.
Given such an order, the associated Weyl vector
\rho^{\lhd}\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
is uniquely defined by

(3.33)

\rho^{\lhd}(\mathfrak{s}_{j}D_{j}^{(1)})=(\rho^{\lhd},\varepsilon_{j})=-\kappa_{j}

for all
j\in{\mathbb{I}}
, where
\kappa_{j}
is defined in (
2.44
).

The parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
uniquely determines a normal order
\lhd
on
{\mathbb{I}}
as follows.
We first insert
\lhd
between each
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}
, then replace those
\{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\,|\,\mathfrak{s}_{i}=1\}
by
1,\ldots,M
and those
\{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\,|\,\mathfrak{s}_{i}=-1\}
by
\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{N}
from left to right.
Conversely, if a normal ordering of
{\mathbb{I}}
is given, it is easy to recover the corresponding parity sequence where any number without a bar corresponds to 1 and any number with a bar corresponds to
-1
.
For example,
\mathfrak{s}=(1,1,-1,1,-1,1)
corresponds to the normal order
1\lhd 2\lhd\overline{1}\lhd 3\lhd\overline{2}\lhd 4
.

Let
\mathfrak{b}^{\lhd}
be the Borel subalgebra spanned by
\{e_{i,j}\,|\,i\unlhd j\}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{+}^{\lhd}
= span
\{e_{i,j}\,|\,i\lhd j\}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}^{\lhd}
= span
\{e_{j,i}\,|\,i\lhd j\}
.
It follows from the PBW theorem that the direct sum decomposition

(3.34)

\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})=S(\mathfrak{c})\oplus(\mathfrak{n}_{-}^{\lhd}\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})+\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\mathfrak{n}_{+}^{\lhd})

induces an algebra homomorphism
\phi^{\lhd}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{c})
. The Harish-Chandra homomorphism is defined by the restriction of
\phi^{\lhd}
on
Z(\mathfrak{g})
followed by an automorphism

(3.35)

\operatorname{HC}:=S_{-\rho^{\lhd}}\circ\phi^{\lhd}:Z(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{c}),

where
S_{-\rho^{\lhd}}:S(\mathfrak{c})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{c})
is induced by

(3.36)

S_{-\rho^{\lhd}}(e_{i,i})=e_{i,i}+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\kappa_{i},\qquad\forall\,1\leqslant i\leqslant M+N.

Similar to the notation
z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}(u)
from (
2.48
), we shall write
\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}}
when we want to emphasize which parity sequence we are using in the definition of the Harish-Chandra homomorphism.
We recall some well-known results as the following theorem.

Theorem 3.17

(
[
5
, Thms 2.2–2.4]
[
11
, §2.2.3]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be an arbitrary parity sequence of type
(M|N)
. We have the following.

(1)

The homomorphism
\operatorname{HC}:Z(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow I(\mathfrak{c})
is an algebra isomorphism.

(2)

The map
\operatorname{HC}:Z(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{c})
is independent of the choice of the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
(equivalently, independent of the choice of the normal order
\lhd
).

(3)

We have

\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}}(z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}(u))=\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{I}}}(u+\omega_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}=\prod_{k=1}^{M}(u+x_{k})\bigg/\prod_{t=1}^{N}(u+y_{t}),

where the product in the middle expression is written from left to right increasingly with respect to the normal ordering on
{\mathbb{I}}
determined by
\mathfrak{s}
, and
\omega_{j}=e_{j,j}=x_{j^{\prime}}
if
\mathfrak{s}_{j}=1
and
\omega_{j}=-e_{j,j}=y_{j^{\prime}}
if
\mathfrak{s}_{j}=-1
with

j^{\prime}:=\#\left\{1\leqslant i<j\>\big|\>\mathfrak{s}_{i}=\mathfrak{s}_{j}\right\}+1.

(4)

In particular, the coefficients
\{z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}^{(r)}\,|\,r\geqslant 1\}
of
z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}(u)
generate the center
Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N}))
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}}(z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}^{(r)})=e_{r}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{M}/y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})
.

Proof.

Part (1) is due to Kac and Sergeev, see
[
36
, §13.2]
for details. Part (2) is established in
[
5
, Thm. 2.2]
while Part (3) for the standard parity sequence is obtained in
[
5
, Thm. 2.3]
. Part (4) follows from Parts (1)-(3).

Part (3) for a general parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
follows from Part (2) and the statement for the standard parity case. Here we provide a more direct approach. By the explicit decomposition of the quantum Berezinian
\mathcal{C}_{M|N}(u)
with respect to an arbitrary parity sequence given in (
2.45
), together with the fact that the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}:\mathrm{Y}_{M|N}\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
from (
2.22
) is a homomorphism, one deduces that

z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}}(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{M+N}(u-\kappa_{i})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\operatorname{ev}(\mathcal{C}_{M|N}(u)).

By (
3.35
), the Harish-Chandra map
\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{s}
carries the correct translations to each
e_{j,j}
, deducing the desired expression.
∎

Since the normal order
\lhd
is determined by
\mathfrak{s}
, the theorem above implies that no matter how the boxes of the column
\pi
are stacked, we always get the same isomorphism between
Z(\mathfrak{g})
and
I(\mathfrak{c})
, i.e., their images under the Harish-Chandra maps coincide.

Example 3.18
.

Consider
\mathfrak{gl}_{2|2}
. Let
\mathfrak{s}=(1,1,-1,-1)
be the standard one, where
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4})=(0,1,1,0)
. Thus
{\mathbb{I}}=\{1\lhd 2\lhd\overline{1}\lhd\overline{2}\}
, and

z_{2|2,\mathfrak{s}}(u)=\zeta_{1}(u)\zeta_{2}(u-1)\big(\zeta_{\overline{1}}(u-1)\zeta_{\overline{2}}(u)\big)^{-1}=\frac{(u+e_{1,1})(u-1+e_{2,2})}{(u-1-e_{3,3})(u-e_{4,4})}\\
=\frac{(u+(e_{1,1}-0))(u+(e_{2,2}-1))}{(u-(e_{3,3}+1))(u-(e_{4,4}+0))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\frac{(u+e_{1,1})(u+e_{2,2})}{(u-e_{3,3})(u-e_{4,4})}=\frac{(u+x_{1})(u+x_{2})}{(u+y_{1})(u+y_{2})}.

On the other hand, choose
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}=(-1,1,-1,1)
, where
\kappa_{i}=-1
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
.
Thus
{\mathbb{I}}=\{\overline{1}\lhd 1\lhd\overline{2}\lhd 2\}
, and

z_{2|2,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(u)=\big(\zeta_{\overline{1}}(u+1)\big)^{-1}\zeta_{1}(u+1)\big(\zeta_{\overline{2}}(u+1)\big)^{-1}\zeta_{2}(u+1)=\frac{(u+1+e_{1,1})(u+1+e_{2,2})}{(u+1-e_{3,3})(u+1-e_{4,4})}\\
=\frac{(u+(e_{1,1}+1))(u+(e_{2,2}+1))}{(u-(e_{3,3}-1))(u-(e_{4,4}-1))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{HC}^{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\frac{(u+e_{1,1})(u+e_{2,2})}{(u-e_{3,3})(u-e_{4,4})}=\frac{(u+x_{1})(u+x_{2})}{(u+y_{1})(u+y_{2})}.

4.
Representations of shifted super Yangians

In this section, we define category
\mathcal{O}
, highest weight modules, and
q
-characters for shifted super Yangians. Then we classify the finite dimensional irreducible modules for shifted super Yangians associated to the standard parity sequence.

4.1.
Category
\mathcal{O}

Recall the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{c}
of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
spanned by
D_{i}^{(1)}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and the root decomposition. Given a
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we consider it as a
\mathfrak{c}
-module by restriction. For a weight
\alpha\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
, the
weight space
of weight
\alpha
is the subspace of
\mathcal{M}
defined by

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}:=\big\{v\in\mathcal{M}\mid(D_{i}^{(1)}-\alpha(D_{i}^{(1)}))v=0,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n\big\}.

If
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is non-zero, we say that
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is a
weight space
of weight
\alpha
and
\alpha
is a
weight
of
\mathcal{M}
. A non-zero vector
v\in\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is called a
weight vector
of weight
\mathrm{wt}(v):=\alpha
.

Define
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
to be the full subcategory of the category of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-modules, which consists of all
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-modules
\mathcal{M}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is a direct sum of finite dimensional weight subspaces;

(2)

the set of all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is non-zero is contained in a finite union of sets of the form
\mathfrak{D}(\beta):=\{\alpha\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}\mid\alpha\leqslant\beta\}
for
\beta\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{m|n}:=(1+u^{-1}\mathbb{C}[\![u^{-1}]\!])^{m+n}
denote the subset of
(m+n)
-tuple of power series in
u^{-1}
with constant term 1. We call an element in
\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
an
\bm{\ell}
-weight. We write an
\bm{\ell}
-weight in the form
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i}(u))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n}
. Clearly,
\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
is an abelian group with respect to the point-wise multiplication of the tuples. For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, we write
\lambda_{i}^{(r)}
for the coefficient of
u^{-r}
in
\lambda_{i}(u)
. Then the associated weight of
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
is defined by

(4.1)

\varpi(\bm{\lambda}):=\mathfrak{s}_{1}\lambda_{1}^{(1)}\varepsilon_{1}+\mathfrak{s}_{2}\lambda_{2}^{(1)}\varepsilon_{2}+\dots+\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}\lambda_{m+n}^{(1)}\varepsilon_{m+n}\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}.

For a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, the
\bm{\ell}
-weight space
of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
is a subspace of
\mathcal{M}
defined by

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}:=\big\{v\in\mathcal{M}\mid\forall\penalty\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n\text{ and }r>0,\exists\penalty\ p>0\text{ such that }(D_{i}^{(r)}-A_{i}^{(r)})^{p}v=0\big\}.

If
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}
is non-zero, we say that
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}
is an
\bm{\ell}
-weight space of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
. A non-zero vector
v\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}
is called an
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\mathrm{wt}_{\ell}(v):=\bm{\lambda}
.

Note that the weight space of
\mathcal{M}
are finite dimensional and the elements
D_{i}^{(r)}
mutually commute, one has that for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
,

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}\\
\varpi(\bm{\lambda})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}.

Thus
\mathcal{M}
is also a direct sum of its
\bm{\ell}
-weight spaces,
\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus_{\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}}
.

4.2.
Highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight modules

For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, a vector
v
in a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{M}
is called a
highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
if

(1)

E_{i}^{(r)}v=0
for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
r>s_{i,i+1}
;

(2)

D_{i}^{(r)}v=\lambda_{i}^{(r)}v
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
r>0
.

We call
\mathcal{M}
a
highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
if it is generated by such a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector.

Alternatively, it is not hard to see from the Gauss decomposition (
2.32
) that a vector
v
in a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{M}
is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
if and only if
T_{i,j}^{(r)}v=0
for all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n
and
r>s_{i,j}
, and
T_{i,i}^{(r)}v=\lambda_{i}^{(r)}v
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
r>0
.

Write
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
where
\sigma^{\prime}
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
) is a lower (resp. upper) shift matrix.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
be modules over
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
, respectively. If
v\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(resp.
w\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
) is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
(resp.
\bm{\xi}
), then
v\otimes w
is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}\bm{\xi}
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.7
and Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

By using the isomorphism
\iota
from (
2.35
), one can remove the restriction that
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
are triangular.

For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, define the corresponding Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
as the quotient of
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
by the left ideal generated by the elements

\big\{D_{i}^{(r)}-\lambda_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\big\},\qquad\big\{E_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<m+n,r>s_{i,i+1}\big\}.

The Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
, generated by the highest weight vector
w_{\bm{\lambda}}
(which is the quotient image of the identity). Moreover, it is universal among such modules, i.e. all other highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight modules of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
are quotients of
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, the following sets of vectors form bases for the Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
:

(1)

\{xw_{\bm{\lambda}}\mid x\in\mathcal{X}\}
, where
\mathcal{X}
denotes the collection of all supermonomials in the elements
\{F_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{j,i}\}
taken in some fixed order;

(2)

\{yw_{\bm{\lambda}}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
, where
\mathcal{Y}
denotes the collection of all supermonomials in the elements
\{T_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{j,i}\}
taken in some fixed order.

Proof.

The first statement follows immediately from Proposition
2.4
. The second statement follows from similar filtration argument as in
[
8
, Thm. 5.5]
with the help of Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Therefore, any other weight vectors other than
w_{\bm{\lambda}}
have weights smaller than
\mathrm{wt}(w_{\bm{\lambda}})=\varpi(\bm{\lambda})
and the
\bm{\ell}
-weight space of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
in
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is one-dimensional. It follows from the standard argument that
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
has a unique maximal submodule, the
quotient by which is irreducible and denoted by
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is the unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
over
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
. We shall always use
v_{\bm{\lambda}}
to denote a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
in
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
.

If
\sigma
is the zero matrix, then we simply write
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\lambda}):=\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
and
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda}):=\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
.

Clearly,
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is not in the category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
. However, the irreducible
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
can be in
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
with suitable
\bm{\lambda}
. Denote by
\mathscr{R}_{m|n}
the subset of
\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
consisting of all
\bm{\lambda}
such that
\lambda_{i}(u)/\lambda_{i+1}(u)
is a rational function for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
.

By the standard argument in
[
35
, Thm. 3.6]
, one has the following.

Proposition 4.4
.

For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, the
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
has finite dimensional weight spaces (or equivalently
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
) if and only if
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{R}_{m|n}
. Moreover, any simple object in
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
for some
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{R}_{m|n}
. Furthermore, all
\bm{\ell}
-weights of a module in
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
are in
\mathscr{R}_{m|n}
.

The following is a generalization of
[
23
, Prop. 3.8]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\bm{\zeta}
be an
\bm{\ell}
-weight such that

\frac{\zeta_{i}(u)}{\zeta_{i+1}(u)}=\frac{u^{d_{i}}}{Q_{i}(u)}

where
d_{i}:=s_{i,i+1}+s_{i+1,i}
and
Q_{i}(u)
is a monic polynomial in
u
of degree
d_{i}
for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
. Then the
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
is 1-dimensional.

Proof.

Consider the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
w_{\bm{\zeta}}
in the Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
. Then it follows from (
2.10
) and (
2.40
) that

E_{i}^{(r)}F_{i}^{(s)}w_{\bm{\zeta}}=[E_{i}^{(r)},F_{i}^{(s)}]w_{\bm{\zeta}}=-\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}H_{i}^{(r+s-1)}w_{\bm{\zeta}}=0

for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
,
r>s_{i,i+1}
, and
s>s_{i+1,i}
due to our choice of
\bm{\zeta}
. Indeed, by (
2.40
) we have that
H_{i}(u)w_{\bm{\zeta}}=u^{-d_{i}}Q_{i}(u)w_{\bm{\zeta}}
and hence
H_{i}^{(r+s-1)}w_{\bm{\zeta}}=0
if
r>s_{i,i+1}
and
s>s_{i+1,i}
. Thus the unique maximal proper submodule in
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
is generated by all
F_{i}^{(s)}w_{\bm{\zeta}}
for
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
s>s_{i+1,i}
, completing the proof.
∎

4.3.
q
-character

Denote by
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathscr{P}_{m|n}]
the
completed group algebra
which consists of formal sums

S=\sum_{\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}}a_{\bm{\lambda}}[\bm{\lambda}]

for integers
a_{\bm{\lambda}}
satisfying

(1)

the set
\{\varpi(\bm{\lambda})\mid\bm{\lambda}\in\mathrm{supp}\,S\}
is contained in a finite union of sets of the form
\mathfrak{D}(\beta)
for
\beta\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
;

(2)

for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
the set
\{\bm{\lambda}\in\mathrm{supp}\,S\mid\varpi(\bm{\lambda})=\alpha\}
is finite.

Here
\mathrm{supp}\,S:=\{\bm{\lambda}\mid a_{\bm{\lambda}}\neq 0\}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathscr{P}_{m|n}]
is multiplicative by extending
[\bm{\lambda}][\bm{\xi}]=[\bm{\lambda}\bm{\xi}]
.

Following
[
24
]
and
[
15
]
(cf. also
[
29
]
for the super Yangian case), define the
q
-character (also called
Gelfand-Tsetlin character
) of a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{M}
in the category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
by

(4.2)

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}:=\sum_{\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}}(\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\lambda}})[\bm{\lambda}].

Since
\mathcal{M}
is in category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
, we have
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathscr{P}_{m|n}]
.

Recall the coproduct
\Delta:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
from (
2.41
), where
\sigma^{\prime}
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
) is the lower (resp. upper) triangular shift matrix such that
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime})
-module and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
a
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
-module. Then the tensor product of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
can be regarded as a
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module via
\Delta
, which we denote it by the “external” tensor product
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\Delta(D_{i}^{(1)})=D_{i}^{(1)}\otimes 1+1\otimes D_{i}^{(1)}
, we have

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{\beta}\subset(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})_{\alpha+\beta}

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{c}^{*}
.

The following lemma is standard, following the same argument of
[
24
, Prop. 1]
and
[
15
, Thm. 2]
with the help of Proposition
2.7
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
. Then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
and

\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})=(\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime})(\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}).

4.4.
Finite dimensional irreducible modules

We first establish some preliminary results for the rank 1 case using the results of
[
4
]
for principal finite
W
-superalgebras.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{1|1}
, the irreducible
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is finite dimensional if and only if
\lambda_{1}(u)/\lambda_{2}(u)
is a rational function.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
). This follows immediately from Proposition
4.4
.

(
\Leftarrow
). Suppose
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{1|1}
is such that
\lambda_{1}(u)/\lambda_{2}(u)
is a rational function. If
\sigma
is the zero matrix, then the statement follows from
[
52
, Thm. 4]
. Now consider the general case. By the canonical embedding
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}(\sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}
, the finite dimensional
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
is also a
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}(\sigma)
-module by restriction. Moreover,
v_{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
remains to be a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
over
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}(\sigma)
. Thus
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is a sub-quotient of
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
and hence finite dimensional as well.
∎

In the rest of this subsection, we write that
d:=s_{1,2}+s_{2,1}
. By Proposition
4.7
, we can write

(4.3)

\frac{\lambda_{1}(u)}{\lambda_{2}(u)}=\frac{f(u)}{g(u)}=\frac{u^{d}P(u)}{Q(u)},

where
f(u)
,
g(u)
,
P(u)
and
Q(u)
are monic polynomials in
u
such that

(4.4)

(f(u),g(u))=(P(u),Q(u))=1.

This is always possible by taking
P(u)=f(u)/(f(u),u^{d})
and
Q(u)=g(u)u^{d}/(f(u),u^{d})
.

Let
r=\deg P(u)
, then
\deg Q(u)=d+r
. We write further that

(4.5)

P(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(u+a_{i}),\qquad Q(u)=\prod_{j=1}^{d+r}(u+b_{j}),

where
a_{i},b_{j}
are complex numbers. Let
v_{\bm{\lambda}}
be a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector in
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Under the above assumption, we have the following.

(1)

The dimension of
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is
2^{r}
with a basis given by

(4.6)

\{F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\cdots F_{1}^{(i_{k})}v_{\bm{\lambda}}\mid 0\leqslant k\leqslant r,s_{2,1}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leqslant s_{2,1}+r\}.

(2)

If
d>0
, then
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is isomorphic to the restriction of the
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
if and only if
f(0)\neq 0
.

Proof.

(1). Recall
\mu_{\wp}
from (
2.36
) which fixes
F_{1}^{(r)}
for all
r>0
. Twisting the module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
by the automorphism
\mu_{\wp}
with suitable
\wp(u)
, we can assume

(4.7)

\lambda_{1}(u)=u^{-r}P(u),\qquad\lambda_{2}(u)=u^{-d-r}Q(u).

Let
W(\pi)
be the corresponding finite
W
-superalgebra obtained via the quotient
\Omega
. It follows from
[
4
, §7]
that there exists an irreducible
W
-module denoted by
\overline{L}(\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\cdots a_{r}\\
b_{1}\cdots b_{d+r}\end{subarray})
. Pulling back through the quotient homomorphism
\Omega
,
\overline{L}(\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\cdots a_{r}\\
b_{1}\cdots b_{d+r}\end{subarray})
remains to be irreducible as a
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}(\sigma)
-module. Moreover, it is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module with highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
. Hence it is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
and the statement follows from
[
4
, Thm. 7.3]
.

(2). If
f(0)\neq 0
, then
(f(u),u^{d})=1
and hence
f(u)=P(u)
. This implies
r=\deg f(u)
. It is known from
[
52
, Thm. 4]
that the
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
has dimension
2^{r}
. Thus it follows from Part (1) that
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
and the
\mathrm{Y}_{1|1}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
have the same dimension. The statement follows as
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is a sub-quotient of the restriction module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
.
∎

Now we classify finite dimensional irreducible modules over the shifted super Yangian
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
associated with the standard parity sequence. We remark that such classification for non-shifted case is only available for standard parity in
[
53
, Thm. 4]
. Though a criterion for
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\lambda})
to be finite dimensional with an arbitrary parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}
can be
recursively deduced from
[
53
, Thm. 4]
via the odd reflections of super Yangian, see
[
34
,
31
]
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be the standard parity sequence. For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, the irreducible
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is finite dimensional if and only if there exist (necessarily unique) monic polynomials
P_{i}(u),Q_{i}(u)
for
1\leqslant i<m+n
such that
\deg P_{m}(u)=\deg Q_{m}(u)
,
(P_{i}(u),Q_{i}(u))=1
,
Q_{j}(u)
is of degree
d_{j}:=s_{j,j+1}+s_{j+1,j}
, and

(4.8)

\frac{\lambda_{j}(u)}{\lambda_{j+1}(u)}=\frac{P_{j}(u)}{P_{j}(u-\mathfrak{s}_{j})}\times\frac{u^{d_{j}}}{Q_{j}(u)},\qquad\frac{\lambda_{m}(u)}{\lambda_{m+1}(u)}=\frac{P_{m}(u)}{Q_{m}(u)},

for each
1\leqslant j<m+n
and
j\neq m
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
). Let
\sigma_{j}
denote the
2\times 2
submatrix

\begin{bmatrix}s_{j,j}&s_{j,j+1}\\
s_{j+1,j}&s_{j+1,j+1}\end{bmatrix}

of the shift matrix
\sigma
. The map
\psi_{j}:\mathrm{Y}_{\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}}(\sigma_{j})\to{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
defined by

\psi_{j}:\mathrm{Y}_{\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}}(\sigma_{j})\to{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)},\quad D_{k}^{(r)}\mapsto D_{j+k-1}^{(r)},\penalty\ E_{1}^{(r)}\mapsto E_{j}^{(r)},\penalty\ F_{1}^{(r)}\mapsto F_{j}^{(r)},\quad k=1,2,

induces an embedding of the shifted (super) Yangian
\mathrm{Y}_{\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}}(\sigma_{j})
into
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
. Then the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
v_{\bm{\lambda}}
remains to be a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}_{j}=(\lambda_{j}(u),\lambda_{j+1}(u))
over the (super)algebra
\mathrm{Y}_{\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}}(\sigma_{j})
. Note that
\mathfrak{s}
is the standard parity sequence. If
1\leqslant j<m+n
and
j\neq m
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{j}=\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}
and hence (
4.8
) with the corresponding restrictions follows from
[
8
, Coro. 7.5]
. If
j=m
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{m}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{m+1}
and hence (
4.8
) follows from Proposition
4.7
.

(
\Leftarrow
). Recalling the isomorphism
\iota
from (
2.35
), the twist of a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\vec{\sigma})
-module by
\iota
is again a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module with the same highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight. Moreover, the twist of a module in
\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\sigma}}
is in
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
. Thus it suffices to prove it for the case when
\sigma
is an upper shift matrix. Then we have the coproduct
\Delta:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}\otimes{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
. Rewrite
\bm{\lambda}
as a product
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\gamma}\bm{\zeta}
such that

\frac{\gamma_{j}(u)}{\gamma_{j+1}(u)}=\frac{P_{j}(u)}{P_{j}(u-\mathfrak{s}_{j})},\quad\frac{\gamma_{m}(u)}{\gamma_{m+1}(u)}=\frac{P_{m}(u)}{Q_{m}(u)},\quad\frac{\zeta_{j}(u)}{\zeta_{j+1}(u)}=\frac{u^{d_{j}}}{Q_{j}(u)},\quad\frac{\zeta_{m}(u)}{\zeta_{m+1}(u)}=1,

for
1\leqslant j<m+n
and
j\neq m
. Via the coproduct, we have the
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\gamma})\boxtimes\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
, where
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\gamma})
is the irreducible
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
-module with highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\gamma}
. Let
v_{\bm{\gamma}}
and
v_{\bm{\zeta}}
be the corresponding highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vectors in
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\gamma})
and
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
, respectively. It follows from Lemma
4.1
that
v_{\bm{\gamma}}\otimes v_{\bm{\zeta}}
is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
. Thus
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is isomorphic to a sub-quotient of
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\gamma})\boxtimes\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
. By
[
53
, Thm. 4]
,
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\gamma})
is finite dimensional. Therefore, the statement follows as
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\zeta})
is 1-dimensional by Lemma
4.5
.
∎

5.
Representations of finite
W
-superalgebras

In this section, we study highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight modules over the superalgebra
W(\pi)
.

5.1.
Preliminaries

We start with notations that will be used to describe the representations of the finite
W
-superalgebra
W(\pi)
, where
\pi
is a fixed pyramid
\pi=(q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell})
of height
\leqslant m+n
. Denote as usual
(p_{1},\dots,p_{m+n})
the tuple of row lengths, and fix a choice of shift matrix
\sigma=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m+n}
.

A
\pi
-tableau
is the pyramid
\pi
with arbitrary complex numbers inserted into each box of
\pi
.
Two
\pi
-tableaux
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are
row equivalent
, denoted by
\mathbf{A}\sim_{\mathrm{row}}\mathbf{B}
, if one can be obtained from the other by permuting entries within rows. Denote by
\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
the set of all row equivalence classes of
\pi
-tableaux. We call elements of
\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
as
row symmetrized
\pi
-tableaux
.

Similarly, two
\pi
-tableaux
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are
column equivalent
, denoted by
\mathbf{A}\sim_{\mathrm{col}}\mathbf{B}
, if one can be obtained from the other by permuting entries in boxes of the same parity within columns.

Let
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
for pyramids
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
. If
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}
are
\pi^{\prime}
-tableau and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
-tableau, respectively. We write
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}
for the
\pi
-tableau obtained by concatenating
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}
.

Sometimes, we also consider finite
W
-superalgebras that are truncated shifted super Yangians associated to standard parity sequence. We call a pyramid
\pi
is
standard
if the first
m
rows are even while the last
n
rows are odd.

When
\pi
is standard, a
\pi
-tableau is
column strict
if, for boxes in the same column from top to bottom, the entries in even boxes are strictly decreasing while the entries in the odd boxes are strictly increasing. Note that the partial order
\geqslant
on
\mathbb{C}
is defined by
a\geqslant b
if

(a-b)\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote
\mathrm{Col}(\pi)
the set of all column strict
\pi
-tableaux.

Define weights,
\bm{\ell}
-weights, highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vectors,
q
-characters and category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
exactly the same as in Section
4
, regarding
W(\pi)
-modules as
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-modules via the quotient map
\Omega:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\twoheadrightarrow W(\pi)
. Then for a module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
, we have
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathscr{P}_{m|n}]
.

Let
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
be a decomposition with
\pi^{\prime}
of level
\ell^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
of level
\ell^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
a
W(\pi^{\prime})
-module and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
a
W(\pi^{\prime\prime})
-module. One defines a
W(\pi)
-module structure on the tensor product
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
through the coproduct
\Delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}
from (
3.22
). We denote this module by
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}
where
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
are the corresponding shift matrices for
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. It follows from Corollary
3.15
and Lemma
4.6
that

\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\boxtimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})=(\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime})(\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}).

5.2.
Highest weight modules

Our main goal is to understand the universal highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
for the superalgebra
W(\pi)
. Such a module is obviously the unique largest quotient of the
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
-module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
on which the kernel of the homomorphism
\Omega
acts as zero. Thus such a
W(\pi)
-module can be defined as
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
. We write
v_{+}
for the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
1\otimes w_{\bm{\lambda}}
in
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
.

Theorem 5.1
.

For
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
, the
W(\pi)
-module
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is non-zero if and only if
u^{p_{i}}\lambda_{i}(u)\in\mathbb{C}[u]
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
. In that case, the following sets of vectors form bases for
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
:

(1)

\{xv_{+}\mid x\in\mathcal{X}\}
, where
\mathcal{X}
denotes the collection of all supermonomials in the elements
\{F_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,s_{j,i}<r\leqslant s_{j,i}+p_{i}\}
taken in some fixed order;

(2)

\{yv_{+}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
, where
\mathcal{Y}
denotes the collection of all supermonomials in the elements
\{T_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,s_{j,i}<r\leqslant s_{j,i}+p_{i}\}
taken in some fixed order.

Proof.

It suffices to prove Part (2) as Part (1) follows by a similar filtration argument in the proof of
[
8
, Thm. 5.5]
. Recall that the Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is the quotient of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
by the left ideal
\mathcal{J}
generated by the elements

\big\{D_{i}^{(r)}-\lambda_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\big\}\cup\big\{E_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,i+1}\big\}.

It is not hard to see from (
2.25
) and the Gauss decomposition (
2.32
) the left ideal
\mathcal{J}
is also equivalently generated by the elements

P:=\big\{T_{i,i}^{(r)}-\lambda_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,r>0\big\}\cup\big\{T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{i,j}\big\}.

Let
Q:=\big\{T_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,r>s_{j,i}\big\}
. Pick any ordering on
P\cup Q
such that the elements of
Q
precede the elements of
P
. Clearly, it follows from Lemma
2.5
and Proposition
4.3
that the ordered supermonomials in the elements
P\cup Q
containing at least one element from
P
form a basis of
\mathcal{J}
.

The
W(\pi)
-module
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
can be identified as the quotient of
W(\pi)
by the image
\bar{\mathcal{J}}
of
\mathcal{J}
under the quotient map
\Omega:{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\to W(\pi)
.

If
\lambda_{i}^{(r)}\neq 0
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
r>p_{i}
(which is exactly
u^{p_{i}}\lambda_{i}(u)\notin\mathbb{C}[u]
), then it follows from Theorem
3.3
that the image of
T_{i,i}^{(r)}-\lambda_{i}^{(r)}
is a unit in
W(\pi)
and hence
W(\pi)\otimes_{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})=0
.

Conversely, suppose
u^{p_{i}}\lambda_{i}(u)\in\mathbb{C}[u]
. Consider two sets

\displaystyle\overline{P}:=

\displaystyle\,\big\{T_{i,i}^{(r)}-\lambda_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,\,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\big\}

\displaystyle\,\cup\big\{T_{i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,\,s_{i,j}<r\leqslant s_{i,j}+p_{i}\big\}

and
\overline{Q}:=\big\{T_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n,\,s_{j,i}<r\leqslant s_{j,i}+p_{i}\big\}
. By Theorem
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, the order supermonomials in elements of
\overline{P}\cup\overline{Q}
containing at least one element from
\overline{P}
form a basis of
\bar{\mathcal{J}}
. Therefore, the image of
\mathcal{Y}
forms a basis for
W(\pi)/\bar{\mathcal{J}}
, completing the proof of Part (2).
∎

Recall
\kappa_{i}
defined in (
2.44
) for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
. Now suppose that
v_{+}
is a non-zero highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector in some
W(\pi)
-module
\mathcal{M}
. It follows from Theorem
5.1
that there exist numbers
(a_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
such that

(5.1)

(u-\kappa_{i})^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})v_{+}=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j})v_{+},\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n.

In this way, the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
v_{+}
defines a row symmetrized
\pi
-tableau
\mathbf{A}
so that the numbers
a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}
are on the
i
-th row of
\pi
. From now on we shall also say the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
v_{+}
is
of type
\mathbf{A}
if the equations (
5.1
) hold.

Conversely, given
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
with entries
a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}
on its
i
-th row. Define the
Verma module

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
over
W(\pi)
by

(5.2)

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}):=W(\pi)\otimes_{{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}}\mathcal{M}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda}),

where
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i}(u))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n}\in\mathscr{P}_{m|n}
satisfies

(5.3)

(u-\kappa_{i})^{p_{i}}\lambda_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j}),\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n.

Then
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is the universal highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of type
\mathbf{A}
(or
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
). It follows from Theorem
5.1
that
v_{+}
is non-zero and hence a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is in the category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
. Again, it has a unique maximal submodule denoted by
\mathrm{rad}\,\mathcal{(}\mathbf{A})
. The quotient

(5.4)

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}):=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})/\mathrm{rad}\,\mathcal{(}\mathbf{A})\cong W(\pi)\otimes_{{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}}\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda}),

is the unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight module of type
\mathbf{A}
. The modules
L(\mathbf{A})
for
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
give a complete set of pairwise non-isomorphic irreducible modules over
W(\pi)
in the category
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}
, cf. Proposition
4.4
.

Example 5.2
.

The trivial
W(\pi)
-module, defined as the restriction of the trivial
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
-module, is isomorphic to the module
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}_{0})
. Here,
\mathbf{A}_{0}
is the tableau with all entries in the
i
-th row equal to
-\kappa_{i}
. Namely, the corresponding highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i}(u))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n}
satisfies
\lambda_{i}(u)=1
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
.

Example 5.3
.

Suppose the pyramid
\pi
consists of a single column of height
m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}\leqslant m+n
, where
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
denote respectively the numbers of even and odd boxes, then we have
W(\pi)=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m^{\prime}|n^{\prime}})
. Let
\mathbf{A}
be a
\pi
-tableau
with entries
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}
from top to bottom. A highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector for
W(\pi)
of type
\mathbf{A}
is a vector such that

(1)

e_{i,j}v^{+}=0
for all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}
;

(2)

e_{i,i}v^{+}=\mathfrak{s}_{m+n-m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}+i}(a_{i}+\kappa_{m+n-m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}+i})v^{+}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}
.

Thus the module
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is exactly the Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\alpha)
over
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m^{\prime}|n^{\prime}})
with some suitable weight
\alpha
.

We end this subsection with the classification of finite dimensional irreducible representations of
W(\pi)
when
\pi
is standard.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\pi
be standard. For
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
, the irreducible
W(\pi)
-module
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})
is finite dimensional if and only
\mathbf{A}
has a representative belonging to
\mathrm{Col}(\pi)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
[
8
, Thm. 7.9]
. For the only if part, one only needs to repeat that argument for
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
i\neq m
using
[
8
, Coro. 7.8]
; see also the proof of Theorem
4.9
. For the if part, it is also the same. Suppose that
\mathbf{A}
has a representative belonging to
\mathrm{Col}(\pi)
. Let

\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
be the columns of this representative.
Since
\pi
is standard and
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is column strict, the irreducible module
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}_{i})
is finite dimensional (see Example
5.3
). By
Lemma
4.1
, the tensor product
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})
is a finite dimensional

W(\pi)
-module containing a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of type
\mathbf{A}
. Hence
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})
being its subquotient is also finite
dimensional.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}
be the
\bm{\ell}
-weight related to the
\pi
-tableau
\mathbf{A}
as in (
4.9
). By Theorems
4.9
and
5.4
, the
W(\pi)
-module
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})
is finite dimensional if and only if the
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
-module
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\bm{\lambda})
is finite dimensional.

5.3.
Character of Verma modules

Introduce the following shorthand notation for some special elements of the completed group algebra
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathscr{P}_{m+n}]
:

(5.5)

y_{i,a}:=1+(a+\kappa_{i})u_{i}^{-1}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
.

A
supertuple

c=(c_{i,j,k})_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
is a tuple that satisfies:

(1)

all
c_{i,j,k}
are natural numbers;

(2)

if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{k}
, then
c_{i,j,k}\leqslant 1
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}
.

One of our main results is the character formula of the Verma module.

Theorem 5.6
.

For
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
with entries
a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}
on the
i
-th row for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
, we have

(5.6)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})\\
=\,&\sum_{c}\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\left(y_{i,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,i+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}\prod_{k=i+1}^{m+n}\Big(\frac{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{k}}\right),\end{split}

where the sum is over all supertuples
c=(c_{i,j,k})_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
.

We shall prove Theorem
5.6
in the next two subsections. For the sake of the argument, we shall
assume henceforth that the shift matrix
\sigma
is upper triangular
(or equivalently, that
\pi
is left-justified
). The general case follows immediately by twisting with the isomorphism
\iota
defined in (
2.35
).

By interchanging the two products on the right-hand side of (
5.6
), one obtains the following generalization of
[
8
, Coro. 6.3]
.

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
be the columns of any representative of the row-symmetrized
\pi
-tableau
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
, so that
\mathbf{A}\sim_{\mathrm{row}}\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
. Then

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})=\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{k})=\mathrm{ch}\big(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})\big).

As an application, Corollary
5.7
allows us to establish an isomorphism between centers of the enveloping superalgebra
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{M|N})
and finite
W
-superalgebra
W(\pi)
in Theorem
5.16
below. Corollary
5.7
is also a key ingredient of
[
12
]
for establishing a canonical basis character formula for the irreducible
W(\pi)
-modules in arbitrary parabolic BGG-type categories. This latter application, suggested by S.-J. Cheng and W. Wang, serves as the primary motivation for the present article.

5.4.
Branching rule for generic case

We consider the
generic
case, i.e. under the assumption that
a_{i,j_{1}}-a_{i,j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
1\leqslant j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leqslant p_{i}
. We shall assume this condition holds throughout this subsection and establish the following branching rule for the Verma modules.

Let
\bar{\pi}
the pyramid obtained from
\pi
by removing the bottom row. Then the row lengths of
\bar{\pi}
are
p_{1},\dots,p_{m+n-1}
. The submatrix
\bar{\sigma}=(s_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j<m+n}
corresponds to the shift matrix for
\bar{\pi}
. Clearly, there exists a homomorphism

\displaystyle W(\bar{\pi})\to W(\pi),

\displaystyle D_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto D_{i}^{(r)},

\displaystyle(1\leqslant i<m+n,r>0),

\displaystyle E_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto E_{i}^{(r)},

\displaystyle(1\leqslant i<m+n-1,r>s_{i,i+1}),

\displaystyle F_{i}^{(r)}\mapsto F_{i}^{(r)},

\displaystyle(1\leqslant i<m+n-1,r>s_{i+1,i}).

It follows from Proposition
3.2
that this homomorphism is injective. Therefore, we can consider
W(\bar{\pi})
as a subalgebra of
W(\pi)
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
with entries
a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}
on its
i
-th row for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
, where
a_{i,j_{1}}-a_{i,j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
1\leqslant j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leqslant p_{i}
. There is a filtration
0=M_{0}\subset M_{1}\subset\cdots
of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
as a
W(\bar{\pi})
-module with
\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}M_{i}=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
and subquotients isomorphic to the Verma modules
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B})
for
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{Row}(\bar{\pi})
such that
\mathbf{B}
has the entries
