(a_{i,1}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{i,1}),\dots,(a_{i,p_{i}}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{i,p_{i}})
on its
i
-th row for each
1\leqslant i<m+n
, one for each tuple
(c_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i<m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
of natural numbers such that
c_{i,j}\leqslant 1
if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}
.

This branching rule allows us to prove Theorem
5.6
for generic case, see the beginning of §
5.5
below. Then we deduce Theorem
5.6
for the general case from the generic case by a continuation argument. We prove Theorem
5.8
in the rest of this subsection.

We prepare some lemmas that will be used in the proof.

Lemma 5.9
.

Assume
\pi
is left-justified. The following relations hold in
W(\pi)
.

(1)

For all
i<j
,

•

if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=\mathfrak{s}_{j}
, we have
[F_{j,i}(u)D_{i}(u),F_{j,i}(v)D_{i}(v)]=0
;

•

if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{j}
, we have

(u-v-\mathfrak{s}_{j})F_{j,i}(u)D_{i}(u)F_{j,i}(v)D_{i}(v)=(v-u-\mathfrak{s}_{j})F_{j,i}(v)D_{i}(v)F_{j,i}(u)D_{i}(u).

(2)

For all
i<j<k
,
(u-v)[F_{k,j}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]
equals

(5.7)

\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{r\geqslant 0}(-1)^{r}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leqslant j\\
i_{r+1}=k\end{subarray}}F_{i_{r+1},i_{r}}(u)F_{i_{r},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)\Big).

(3)

For all
i<j
and
k<i
or
k>j
,
[D_{k}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]=0
.

(4)

For all
i<j
,
(u-v)[D_{i}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]=\mathfrak{s}_{i}(F_{j,i}(u)-F_{j,i}(v))D_{i}(u)
.

(5)

For all
i<j
,
(u-v)[D_{j}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]
equals

\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{r\geqslant 0}(-1)^{r}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}<j\\
i_{r+1}=j\end{subarray}}F_{i_{r+1},i_{r}}(u)F_{i_{r},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)D_{j}(u)\Big).

(6)

For all
i<j<k
,
(-1)^{|i||j|+|i||k|+|j||k|}(u-v)[D_{j}(u),F_{k,i}(v)]
equals

\sum_{r\geqslant 0}(-1)^{r}\Big(\sum_{i<i_{1}<\dots<i_{r}<i_{r+1}=j}F_{i_{r+1},i_{r}}(u)F_{i_{r},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)F_{k,j}(u)D_{j}(u)\Big).

(7)

For all
i<j<k
,
(-1)^{|i||j|+|i||k|+|j||k|}(u-v)[F_{k,j}(u)D_{j}(u),F_{k,i}(v)]
equals

\sum_{r\geqslant 0}(-1)^{r}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}<j\\
i_{r+1}=k\end{subarray}}F_{i_{r+1},i_{r}}(u)F_{i_{r},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)F_{k,j}(u)D_{j}(u)\Big).

Proof.

The proof follows directly from results of
[
40
]
together with some simple inductive argument. We prove (
5.7
) in detail and omit the proof for others. Since
\pi
is left-justified,
\sigma
is upper triangular (
s_{j,i}=0
for all
j\geqslant i
). This implies that the element
F_{j,i}^{(r)}\in\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)
can be identified with
\mathsf{F}_{j,i}^{(r)}\in\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}
. Consequently,

F_{j,i}^{(r)}=\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}[F_{j-1}^{(1)},F_{j-1,i}^{(r)}]
for all
j-i\geqslant 2
,
or in terms of generating series

(5.8)

F_{j,i}(u)=\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}[F_{j-1}^{(1)},F_{j-1,i}(u)].

It suffices to establish (
5.7
) in the special case
k=j+1
:

(5.9)

(u-v)[F_{j}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]=\\
\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{r\geqslant 0}(-1)^{r}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leqslant j\\
i_{r+1}=j+1\end{subarray}}F_{i_{r+1},i_{r}}(u)F_{i_{r},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)\Big).

Indeed, applying
[F_{j+1}^{(1)},?]
to both sides of (
5.9
), using the facts that
[F_{j+1}^{(1)},F_{j,i}(v)]=0
, which follows from (
2.15
), and
[F_{j+1}^{(1)},F_{j+1,h}(u)]=\mathfrak{s}_{j+1}F_{j+2,h}(u)
for all
h\leqslant j
, which follows from (
5.8
), one deduces (
5.7
) for
k=j+2
. Applying
[F_{t}^{(1)},?]
successively to the results for
t=j+2,\cdots,k-1
, one obtains (
5.7
) in general.

We prove (
5.9
) by induction on
j-i
. The initial case when
j-i=1
is exactly the result of applying the shift map
\psi_{i-1}
to
[
40
, (6.21)]
, which also implies the following identity:

(5.10)

[F_{j}(u),F_{j-1}^{(1)}]=\mathfrak{s}_{j}\big(F_{j+1,j-1}(u)-F_{j}(u)F_{j-1}(u)\big).

Assume now
j-i\geqslant 2
, then

\displaystyle(u-v)[F_{j}(u),F_{j,i}(v)]=(u-v)\big[F_{j}(u),[F_{j-1}^{(1)},F_{j-1,i}(v)]\big]\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle(u-v)\big[[F_{j}(u),F_{j-1}^{(1)}],F_{j-1,i}(v)\big]\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pf4.6-2}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(u-v)\big[F_{j+1,j-1}(u)-F_{j}(u)F_{j-1}(u),F_{j-1,i}(v)\big]\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}\mathfrak{s}_{j}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle(u-v)\big[F_{j+1,j-1}(u),F_{j-1,i}(v)\big]\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}\mathfrak{s}_{j}-(u-v)F_{j}(u)\big[F_{j-1}(u),F_{j-1,i}(v)\big]\mathfrak{s}_{j-1}\mathfrak{s}_{j}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{a\geqslant 0}(-1)^{a}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{a}\leqslant j-1\\
i_{a+1}=j+1\end{subarray}}F_{i_{a+1},i_{a}}(u)F_{i_{a},i_{a-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{b\geqslant 0}(-1)^{b}F_{j}(u)\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{b}\leqslant j-1\\
i_{b+1}=j\end{subarray}}F_{i_{b+1},i_{b}}(u)F_{i_{b},i_{r-1}}(u)\cdots F_{i_{2},i_{1}}(u)\big(F_{i_{1},i}(v)-F_{i_{1},i}(u)\big)\Big)

Note that the last equality is due to the induction hypothesis. The two summations correspond to the cases when
i_{r}\neq j
and
i_{r}=j
, respectively, in the RHS of (
5.9
), completing the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

For
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m+n
, if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{j}
, then

F_{ji}(u)D_{i}(u)F_{ji}(u-\mathfrak{s}_{i})D_{i}(u-\mathfrak{s}_{i})=0.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Part (1) of Lemma
5.9
.
∎

Let

(5.11)

L_{i}(u)=\sum_{r=1}^{p_{i}}L_{i}^{(r)}u^{p_{i}-r}:=u^{p_{i}}T_{m+n,i}(u)\in W(\pi)[u]

for each
1\leqslant i<m+n
.
We shall apply the following observation repeatedly from now on: given a vector
\mathfrak{m}
of weight
\alpha
in a
W(\pi)
-module
\mathfrak{M}
with the property that
\alpha+\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{i}
is not a weight of
\mathfrak{M}
for any
1\leqslant j<i
, then we have by (
2.32
) that

L_{i}(u)\mathfrak{m}=u^{p_{i}}F_{m+n,i}(u)D_{i}(u)\mathfrak{m}.

Lemma 5.11
.

Suppose we are given
1\leqslant i<m+n
and a vector
\mathfrak{m}
of weight
\alpha
in a
W(\pi)
-module
\mathfrak{M}
such that

(1)

\alpha-d(\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{m+n})+\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{i}
is not a weight of
\mathfrak{M}
for any
1\leqslant j<i
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(2)

u^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u)\mathfrak{m}\equiv(u+a_{1})\cdots(u+a_{p_{i}})\mathfrak{m}\pmod{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}[u]}
for some
a_{1},\dots,a_{p_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
and some subspace
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{M}
, where
a_{j_{1}}-a_{j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.

For
1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}
, define
\mathfrak{m}_{j}:=L_{i}(-a_{j})\mathfrak{m}
. Then we have

\displaystyle u^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u)\mathfrak{m}_{j}

\displaystyle\equiv\penalty\

\displaystyle(u+a_{1})\cdots(u+a_{j-1})(u+a_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i})(u+a_{j+1})\cdots(u+a_{p_{i}})\mathfrak{m}_{j}\pmod{\sum_{r=1}^{p_{i}}L_{i}^{(r)}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}[u]}.

Moreover, the subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
spanned by the vectors
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{m}_{p_{i}}
coincides with the subspace spanned by the vectors
L_{i}^{(1)}\mathfrak{m},\dots,L_{i}^{(p_{i})}\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to that of
[
8
, Lem. 6.16]
with the help of Part (4) from Lemma
5.9
, which clearly shows how
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
shows up.
∎

Let
C_{d}
denote the set all
p_{i}
-tuples
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{p_{i}})
of natural numbers summing to
d
. Put a total order on
C_{d}
so that
c^{\prime}<c
if
c^{\prime}
is lexicographically greater than
c
. If
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}
, we suppose further that
c_{j}\leqslant 1
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
5.11
, define the vector
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
for
c\in C_{d}
by

\mathfrak{m}_{c}:=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\prod_{k=1}^{c_{j}}L_{i}\big(-a_{j}+\mathfrak{s}_{i}(k-1)\big)\mathfrak{m},

where the products are taken in order of increasing indices from left to right. Then we have

u^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u)\mathfrak{m}_{c}\equiv(u+a_{1}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{1})\cdots(u+a_{p_{i}}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{p_{i}})\mathfrak{m}_{c}\quad\pmod{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{c}[u]}

where
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{c}
is the subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
spanned by all the vectors
\mathfrak{m}_{c^{\prime}}
for
c^{\prime}<c
and
L_{i}^{(r_{1})}\cdots L_{i}^{(r_{d})}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
for
1\leqslant r_{1},\dots,r_{d}\leqslant p_{i}
, Moreover, the vectors
\{\mathfrak{m}_{c}\mid c\in C_{d}\}
span the same subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
as the vectors
L_{i}^{(r_{1})}\cdots L_{i}^{(r_{d})}\mathfrak{m}
for all
1\leqslant r_{1},\dots,r_{d}\leqslant p_{i}
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}
, then the vectors
\{\mathfrak{m}_{c}\mid c\in C_{d}\}
span the same subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
as the vectors
L_{i}^{(r_{1})}\cdots L_{i}^{(r_{d})}\mathfrak{m}
for all
1\leqslant r_{1}<\dots<r_{d}\leqslant p_{i}
.

Proof.

We first note the following simple observation. If
\mathfrak{s}_{i}=\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}
, then by Part (1) of Lemma
5.9
the definition of the vectors
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
does not depend on the order taken in the products. If
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{m+n}
, then by Part (1) of Lemma
5.9
the definition of
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
does depend on the order taken in the products. However, under our genericness assumption, it follows from Part (1) of Lemma
5.9
that different orders in the products give rise to proportional vectors
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
. The rest of the proof is similar (actually slightly simpler as
a_{i}
are generic) to that of
[
8
, Lem. 6.17]
.∎

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
5.8
.
Denote by
C
the set of all supertuples of natural numbers
c=(c_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i<m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
. Let

|c|_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{p_{i}}c_{i,j},\qquad|c|:=\sum_{i=1}^{m+n-1}|c|_{i}.

Introduce a total order on
C
as follows. We say that
c^{\prime}<c
if one of the following hold:

(1)

|c^{\prime}|<|c|
;

(2)

|c^{\prime}|=|c|
and there exists
1\leqslant i<m+n
such that
|c^{\prime}|_{i}>|c|_{i}
and
|c^{\prime}|_{j}=|c|_{j}
for
i<j<m+n
;

(3)

|c^{\prime}|_{j}=|c|_{j}
for all
1\leqslant j<m+n
, and there exists
1\leqslant i<m+n
such that the tuple
(c_{j,1}^{\prime},\dots,c_{j,p_{j}}^{\prime})
is equal to the tuple
(c_{j,1},\dots,c_{j,p_{j}})
for all
1\leqslant j<i
while the tuple
(c_{i,1}^{\prime},\dots,c_{i,p_{i}}^{\prime})
is lexicographically greater than the tuple
(c_{i,1},\dots,c_{i,p_{i}})
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}:=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
. For every supertuple
c\in C
, define a vector
\mathfrak{m}_{c}\in\mathfrak{M}
by

\mathfrak{m}_{c}:=\prod_{i=1}^{m+n-1}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\prod_{k=1}^{c_{i,j}}L_{i}(-a_{i,j}+\mathfrak{s}_{i}(k-1))\,v_{+}

where the products are taken in increasing order from left to right. It follows from Theorem
5.1
and Lemma
5.12
that the vectors
\{y\mathfrak{m}_{c}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y},c\in C\}
form a basis of
\mathfrak{M}
, where
\mathcal{Y}
is the set of all supermonomials in the elements
\{T_{j,i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i<j<m+n,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\}
taken in some fixed order.

For every supertuple
c\in C
, let

\mathfrak{M}_{c}:=\mathrm{span}\{y\mathfrak{m}_{c^{\prime}}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y},c^{\prime}\leqslant c\},\quad\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{span}\{y\mathfrak{m}_{c^{\prime}}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y},c^{\prime}<c\}.

Then
\mathfrak{M}=\bigcup_{c\in C}\mathfrak{M}_{c}
. To complete the proof, it suffices to prove that
\mathfrak{M}_{c}
is a
W(\bar{\pi})
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}
with
\mathfrak{M}_{c}/\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B})
for
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{Row}(\bar{\pi})
such that the
i
-th row of
\mathbf{B}
is
(a_{i,1}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{i,p_{i}})
for
1\leqslant i<m+n
. We prove it by induction on the total order on
C
. By induction hypothesis,
\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}
is a
W(\bar{\pi})
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}
. The vectors

\big\{y\mathfrak{m}_{c^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}\mid y\in\mathcal{Y},c^{\prime}\geqslant c\big\}.

form a basis of the
W(\bar{\pi})
-module
\mathfrak{M}/\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}
. It follows from the definition of the total order on
C
that the vector
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{c}:=\mathfrak{m}_{c}+\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}
is a vector of maximal
\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n}
-weight in
\mathfrak{M}/\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}
. Therefore, the vector
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{c}
is annihilated by all
E_{i}^{(r)}
for
1\leqslant i<m+n-1
and
r>s_{i,i+1}
. By Parts (3), (6), (7) of Lemma
5.9
, Lemma
5.12
, and the PBW theorem for
\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}^{\leqslant 0}(\sigma)
, we have

u^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u)\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{c}=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}c_{i,j})\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{c}.

Thus,
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{c}
is a highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight corresponding to the pyramid
\bar{\pi}
. By Part (2) of Theorem
5.1
and the universal property of the Verma modules, we conclude that
\mathfrak{M}_{c}
is a
W(\bar{\pi})
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}
and moreover
\mathfrak{M}_{c}/\mathfrak{M}_{c}^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B})
as they have the same size.

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.6

We first prove Theorem
5.6
under the genericness assumption, i.e.
a_{i,j_{1}}-a_{i,j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
and
1\leqslant j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leqslant p_{i}
. We proceed by induction on
m+n
. The base case
m+n=1
is trivial. For the inductive step, it follows from Theorem
5.8
and the induction hypothesis that the character of
\mathrm{Res}_{W(\bar{\pi})}^{W(\pi)}\mathcal{(}\mathbf{A})
equals

\sum_{c}\prod_{i=1}^{m+n-1}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\left(y_{i,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,i+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}\prod_{k=i+1}^{m+n-1}\Big(\frac{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{k}}\right),

where the sum is over all supertuples
c=(c_{i,j,k})_{1\leqslant i<k<m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
.

Note that by Proposition
2.9
the operator

\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\Big((u-\kappa_{i})^{p_{i}}D_{i}(u-\kappa_{i})\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}

acts on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
as the scalar
\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
. Recall from (
5.5
), every monomial appearing in
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
has to simplify to
\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}(u+a_{i,j})^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
if we replace
y_{i,a}
by
(u+a)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
everywhere. Therefore, one recovers
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
uniquely from the above expression, completing the proof of Theorem
5.6
under the genericness assumption.

Now let us consider the general case where
a_{i,j_{1}}-a_{i,j_{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}
might happen for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
. Fix an arbitrary tuple of complex numbers
\varsigma=(\varsigma_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i<m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
such that

(5.12)

(a_{i,j_{1}}+\varsigma_{i,j_{1}}t)-(a_{i,j_{2}}+\varsigma_{i,j_{2}}t)\notin\mathbb{Z}

for all
1\leqslant i<m+n
,
1\leqslant j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leqslant p_{2}
, and
t\in(0,1]
. We write
a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(t):=a_{i,j}+\varsigma_{i,j}t
and
\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(t)
for the symmetrized
\pi
-tableau with
(a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(t))_{1\leqslant i<m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
in its
i
-th row. Clearly, we have
a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(0)=a_{i,j}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(0)=\mathbf{A}
.

Define an order on the elements
\bm{F}:=\{F_{k,i}^{(j)}\mid 1\leqslant i<k\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}\}
by the lexicographical order of the tuple
(k,i,j)
. Fix an arbitrary order for the elements
\bm{D}:=\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant r\leqslant p_{i}\}
. Extend the orders so that the
F
’s are always smaller than the
D
’s.

Let
W^{-}(\pi)
and
W^{0}(\pi)
be the subalgebras of
W(\pi)
generated by elements from
\bm{F}
and
\bm{D}
, respectively. Let
W^{\leqslant 0}(\pi)
be the subalgebra of
W(\pi)
generated by elements from
\bm{F}\cup\bm{D}
.

For a supertuple
\theta=(\theta_{i,j,k})_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
, define the ordered monomial

F_{\theta}=\prod_{k=2}^{m+n}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\big(F_{k,i}^{(j)}\big)^{\theta_{i,j,k}}.

Then it follows from Proposition
3.2
that the ordered monomials
F_{\theta}
for all supertuples
\theta
form a basis of
W^{-}(\pi)
, where we put elements of smaller order to the left of larger ones. Similarly, we have a PBW type basis for
W^{\leqslant 0}(\pi)
. Note that
W(\pi)
is
\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}
-graded. For
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}^{+}
, denote by
C_{\alpha}
the set of all supertuples
\theta
such that the degree of
F_{\theta}
is
-\alpha
. Clearly,
C_{\alpha}
is a finite set. Since
\deg D_{i}^{(r)}=0
, by (
2.9
) and the PBW basis, there exist unique constants
\Gamma^{(r,s)}_{i;\theta,\theta^{\prime}}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
,
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in C_{\alpha}
and
1\leqslant r,s\leqslant p_{i}
such that

(5.13)

D_{i}^{(r)}F_{\theta}=\sum_{s=1}^{p_{i}}\sum_{\theta^{\prime}\in C_{\alpha}}\Gamma^{(r,s)}_{i;\theta,\theta^{\prime}}F_{\theta^{\prime}}D_{i}^{(s)}.

For the Verma modules
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(t))
, it follows from Theorem
5.1
that we can identify all these modules with the same vector superspace
W^{-}(\pi)
. Due to (
5.1
), for each
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}_{m|n}^{+}
, the subspace

W_{\alpha}^{-}(\pi):=\mathrm{span}\{F_{\theta}\mid\theta\in C_{\alpha}\}

is invariant under the action of
D_{i}^{(r)}
. It follows from (
5.1
) and (
5.13
) that the element
D_{i}^{(r)}
acts on the vector
F_{\theta}
by

D_{i}^{(r)}F_{\theta}=\sum_{s=1}^{p_{i}}\sum_{\theta^{\prime}\in C_{\alpha}}\Gamma^{(r,s)}_{i;\theta,\theta^{\prime}}F_{\theta^{\prime}}e_{s}\big(a_{i,1}^{\varsigma}(t)+\kappa_{i},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}^{\varsigma}(t)+\kappa_{i}\big),

where
e_{s}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})
is the
s
-th elementary symmetric polynomials. We can also think this is obtained from (
5.13
) applying to the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector
v_{+}
. Hence all the entries of the matrix corresponding to the action of
D_{i}^{(r)}
on
W_{\alpha}^{-}(\pi)\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(t))
with respect to the basis
\mathrm{span}\{F_{\theta}\mid\theta\in C_{\alpha}\}
are polynomials in
t
. Moreover, it follows from (
5.12
)
and the generic case established above that the
q
-character of
\mathrm{ch}\big(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(t))\big)
for
t\in(0,1]
restricted to the subspace
W_{\alpha}^{-}(\pi)
is given by

\sum_{c\in C_{\alpha}}\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\left(y_{i,a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(t)-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,i+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}\prod_{k=i+1}^{m+n}\Big(\frac{y_{k,a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(t)-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}{y_{k,a_{i,j}^{\varsigma}(t)-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{k}}\right).

It is well known that the eigenvalues of a matrix depend continuously on its entries, see e.g.
[
1
, Coro. VI.1.6]
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
t\to X(t)
be a continuous map from
[0,1]
into the space of
r\times r
complex matrices. Then there exist continuous functions
\lambda_{1}(t),\dots,\lambda_{r}(t)
that, for each
t\in[0,1]
, are eigenvalues of
X(t)
.

Note that we know explicitly the generalized joint eigenvalues of
\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\}
acting on the subspace
W_{\alpha}^{-}(\pi)
for
t\in(0,1]
. By applying Lemma
5.13
to all possible linear combinations of the elements
\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\}
, we concluded that the generalized joint eigenvalues (with multiplicities) of
\{D_{i}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n,0<r\leqslant p_{i}\}
acting on the subspace
W_{\alpha}^{-}(\pi)
for
t=0
are encoded by

\sum_{c\in C_{\alpha}}\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\left(y_{i,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,i+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}\prod_{k=i+1}^{m+n}\Big(\frac{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{k}}\right).

Thus
we have
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\mathrm{ch}\big(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}^{\varsigma}(t))\big)
, completing the proof for the general case.

5.6.
Isomorphism of Center

As an application of the character formula, we show that the center of
W
-superalgebra depends only on the superdimension
(M|N)
up to isomorphism, and is independent of the shape of the pyramid.
Our approach combines the arguments in
[
8
,
5
]
.

The following simple lemma shows that generic Verma modules are irreducible.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
with entries
a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}
on its
i
-th row for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
, where
a_{i_{1},j_{1}}-a_{i_{2},j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i_{1},i_{2}\leqslant m+n
and
1\leqslant j_{1},j_{2}\leqslant p_{i}
provided
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})
. Then we have the following.

(1)

The Verma module
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is irreducible.

(2)

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
be the columns of any representative of the row-symmetrized
\pi
-tableau
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
, so that
\mathbf{A}\sim_{\mathrm{row}}\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
. Then

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell}).

Proof.

Part (2) follows from Part (1) and Corollary
5.7
. Thus it suffices to prove Part (1).

Suppose
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
is reducible, then we can assume
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})
is a subquotient of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})
, where
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{Row}(\pi)
with entries
b_{i,1},\dots,b_{i,p_{i}}
on its
i
-th row for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
and
\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}
. Therefore the
\bm{\ell}
-weight corresponding to the highest
\bm{\ell}
-weight vector of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})
is
\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}y_{i,b_{i,j}}
. Hence there exists a supertuple
c=(c_{i,j,k})_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant m+n,1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}}
such that

\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}y_{i,b_{i,j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m+n}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\left(y_{i,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,i+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}\prod_{k=i+1}^{m+n}\Big(\frac{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k+1}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}{y_{k,a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}(c_{i,j,k}+\dots+c_{i,j,m+n})}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{k}}\right).

Since
\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}
, the supertuple
c
is non-trivial. Let
{\mathfrak{i}}
be maximal such that
c_{i,j,{\mathfrak{i}}}\neq 0
for some
1\leqslant i<{\mathfrak{i}}
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant p_{i}
. Then we have

\prod_{j=1}^{p_{\mathfrak{i}}}y_{{\mathfrak{i}},b_{{\mathfrak{i}},j}}=\prod_{j=1}^{p_{\mathfrak{i}}}y_{{\mathfrak{i}},a_{{\mathfrak{i}},j}}\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{i}}-1}\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\Big(\frac{y_{{\mathfrak{i}},a_{i,j}}}{y_{{\mathfrak{i}},a_{i,j}-\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{i}}c_{i,j,{\mathfrak{i}}}}}\Big)^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{i}}}.

Since
a_{i_{1},j_{1}}-a_{i_{2},j_{2}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})
, there is no way to rewrite the RHS as a monomial in
y_{{\mathfrak{i}},b_{{\mathfrak{i}},j}}
as the LHS. Hence we obtain a contradiction, completing the proof.
∎

Recall the notations from Section
3
and, in particular, the Miura transform
\mu
from (
3.23
).

Lemma 5.15
.

We have
\mu\big(Z(W(\pi))\big)\subset Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}))
.

Proof.

We need to show that
[\mu(z),h]=0
for
z\in Z(W(\pi))
and
h\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
. By the standard fact that the annihilator of any Zariski dense set of Verma (super)modules is zero (see e.g.
[
36
, Proof of Lem. 13.1.4]
), it suffices to check that
[\mu(z),h]
annihilates
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})
for generic
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Tab}(\pi)
with columns
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
. By Lemma
5.14
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})
is irreducible for generic
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{Tab}(\pi)
when viewed as a
W(\pi)
-module via pulling back through
\mu
. Therefore
\mu(z)
acts on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})
as a scalar, implying that
[\mu(z),h]
acts on it as zero.
∎

Let
\mathsterling:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
be the superalgebra automorphism given by

\mathsterling(e_{ij})=\tilde{e}_{i,j}=e_{ij}+\delta_{i,j}\mathfrak{s}_{\operatorname{row}(i)}(\hbar-\check{q}_{\operatorname{col}(i)}-\check{q}_{\operatorname{col}(i)+1}-\cdots-\check{q}_{\ell}),

where
\hbar=m-n
and
\check{q}_{a}=m_{a}-n_{a}
, for all
1\leqslant a\leqslant\ell
. Let
\psi:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
be the linear map defined as the composition first of the projection

\mathrm{pr}_{\chi}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})
along the direct sum decomposition
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{p})\oplus I_{\chi}
then the automorphism
\mathsterling
. The restriction of
\psi
to
Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))
gives a well-defined algebra homomorphism

(5.14)

\psi:Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))\to Z(W(\pi)),

where the image belongs to the center of
W(\pi)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}(\pi)
be the parity sequence obtained from reading the parity of boxes of
\pi
down-columns from left to right.
Recall the series
z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}(u)
defined in (
2.48
).
We calculate the image of
z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}(u)
under the composition
\mu\circ\psi:Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))\to\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
as follows.
Since the Miura map
\mu
can be understood as the iteration of the parabolic induction (
3.22
) by
(\ell-1)
times, the following result can be deduced as in the one-column case discussed in §
3.5
:

(5.15)

\begin{split}\mu\circ\psi\big(z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}(u)\big)=&\,\mathsterling_{1}\big(z_{m_{1}|n_{1},\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{1})}(u)\big)\otimes\mathsterling_{2}\big(z_{m_{2}|n_{2},\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{2})}(u-\check{q_{1}})\big)\otimes\cdots\\
&\,\cdots\otimes\mathsterling_{\ell}\big(z_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell},\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{\ell})}(u-\check{q_{1}}-\check{q_{2}}-\cdots-\check{q}_{\ell-1})\big)\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})[\![u^{-1}]\!],\end{split}

where
\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{a})
stands for the parity sequence obtained from the reading of the
a
-th column of
\pi
and
\mathsterling_{a}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{a}|n_{a}})\to\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{a}|n_{a}})
is the automorphism sending
e_{i,j}
to
e_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j}(-1)^{\operatorname{pa}(i)}(\hbar-\check{q}_{a})
, for each
1\leqslant a\leqslant\ell
.

Under the identification
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})\cong\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}|n_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{\ell}|n_{\ell}})
, the subspace
S(\mathfrak{c})\subseteq\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})
is identified with

S(\mathfrak{c})\cong S(\mathfrak{c}_{1})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}_{2})\otimes\cdots\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}_{\ell}),

where
\mathfrak{c}_{a}
denotes the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{a}|n_{a}}
, corresponding to the
a
-th column of
\pi
.
Let
\operatorname{hc}_{a}:Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{a}|n_{a}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}I(\mathfrak{c}_{a})\subseteq S(\mathfrak{c}_{a})
denote the Harish-Chandra map with respect to the parity sequence
\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{a})
.
Consider the composition

(5.16)

\operatorname{hc}:=(\operatorname{hc}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{hc}_{\ell})\circ({\mathsterling_{1}}^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathsterling_{\ell}^{-1})}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{c}).

By (
5.15
) and (
5.16
), we see that

\operatorname{HC}^{\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}\big(z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}(u)\big)=\operatorname{hc}\circ\mu\circ\psi\big(z_{M|N,\mathfrak{s}(\pi)}(u)\big).

Together with Lemma
5.15
, the following diagram commutes:

(5.17)

\begin{CD}Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))&&@>{\operatorname{HC}}>{}>&&S(\mathfrak{c})\\
@V{\psi}V{}V&&&&@A{}A{\operatorname{hc}}A\\
Z(W(\pi))&&@>{\mu}>{}>&&Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}))\end{CD}

Theorem 5.16
.

The map
\psi:Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))\to Z(W(\pi))
is an algebra isomorphism. In particular, the elements
\psi(z_{M|N}^{(r)})
for all
r\geqslant 1
generate the center
Z(W(\pi))
.

Proof.

The injectivity of
\psi
follows from that of
\operatorname{HC}
and the commutative diagram (
5.17
).
It remains to prove that
\psi
is surjective. Since
\operatorname{hc}\circ\mu
is injective and we know
\operatorname{HC}\big(Z(\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}))\big)=I(\mathfrak{c})
by Theorem
3.17
, it suffices to show
\operatorname{hc}\circ\mu\big(Z(W(\pi))\big)\subseteq I(\mathfrak{c})
. Note that the image of
\operatorname{hc}
is contained in
I(\mathfrak{c}_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes I(\mathfrak{c}_{\ell})
. It reduces to show that
\operatorname{hc}\circ\mu\big(Z(W(\pi))\big)\subset S(\mathfrak{c})^{\mathfrak{S}_{M}\times\mathfrak{S}_{N}}
as
I(\mathfrak{c})=\big(I(\mathfrak{c}_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes I(\mathfrak{c}_{\ell})\big)\cap\big(S(\mathfrak{c})^{\mathfrak{S}_{M}\times\mathfrak{S}_{N}}\big)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
be
\pi
-tableaux that share the same content in even and odd boxes, respectively. By the definition of the Harish-Chandra homomorphism, to show
\operatorname{hc}\circ\mu\big(Z(W(\pi))\big)\subset S(\mathfrak{c})^{\mathfrak{S}_{M}\times\mathfrak{S}_{N}}
, we need to show that any element
z\in Z(W(\pi))
acts on the modules
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B}_{\ell})
by the same scalar, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
denote the
i
-th column of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, respectively.
Similar to the argument in the proof of Lemma
5.15
, it suffices to prove the statement for generic
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, since the set of generic
\pi
-tableaux is Zariski dense in the set of all
\pi
-tableaux.

If
\mathbf{B}
is obtained from
\mathbf{A}
by permuting even (resp. odd) entries within columns, then this is obvious since the image of
\operatorname{hc}
is contained in
I(\mathfrak{c}_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes I(\mathfrak{c}_{\ell})
.

If
\mathbf{B}
is obtained from
\mathbf{A}
by permuting even (resp. odd) entries within rows, then by (
5.2
)–(
5.3
) we have
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B})
. Since
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are generic, then by Lemma
5.14
we have

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{A})=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B})\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B}_{1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{B}_{\ell}),

as irreducible
W(\pi)
-modules. As a consequence, any element
z\in Z(W(\pi))
acts on these modules by exactly the same scalar. The general case follows from these two special cases.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
3.3

We follow the same strategy of
[
8
, §2.4 & §3.7]
.

A.1.
Parabolic presentation

We recall the parabolic presentations of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
from
[
41
, Proof of Prop. 5.15]
which is slight different from
[
40
]
. By a
shape
we mean a tuple
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{z})
of positive integers summing to
m+n
. We say that a shaper
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{z})
is admissible for
\sigma
if
s_{i,j}=0
for all
\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{a-1}<i,j\leqslant\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{a}
and
1\leqslant a\leqslant z
, in which case we define

(A.1)

s_{a,b}(\nu):=s_{\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{a},\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{b}}

for
1\leqslant a,b\leqslant z
.

Given an admissible shape
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{z})
. Let
{}^{\nu}T_{a,b}(u)
be the
\nu_{a}\times\nu_{b}
matrix whose
ij
-entry is given by
T_{\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{a-1}+i,\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{b-1}+j}(u)
for each
1\leqslant a,b\leqslant z
. Denote
{}^{\nu}T(u)
the
z\times z
-block matrix with the
ab
-block given by
{}^{\nu}T_{a,b}(u)
. Exploit the block-wise Gauss decomposition of
{}^{\nu}T(u)
to define the matrices
{}^{\nu}D(u)
,
{}^{\nu}E(u)
, and
{}^{\nu}F(u)
such that

{}^{\nu}T(u)={}^{\nu}F(u){}^{\nu}D(u){}^{\nu}E(u),

where
{}^{\nu}D(u)
is a
z\times z
diagonal block matrix with
aa
-block
{}^{\nu}D_{a}(u)
being a matrix of size
\nu_{a}\times\nu_{a}
,
{}^{\nu}E(u)
is a
z\times z
upper unitriangular block matrix with
ab
-block
{}^{\nu}E_{a,b}(u)
being a matrix of size
\nu_{a}\times\nu_{b}
, and
{}^{\nu}F(u)
is a
z\times z
lower unitriangular block matrix with
ba
-entry
{}^{\nu}F_{b,a}(u)
being a matrix of size
\nu_{b}\times\nu_{a}
. Thus
{}^{\nu}E_{a,a}(u)={}^{\nu}F_{a,a}(u)=1
are the identity matrix of size
\nu_{a}\times\nu_{a}
for each
1\leqslant a\leqslant z
and

(A.2)

^{\nu}T_{a,b}(u)=\sum_{c=1}^{\min(a,b)}{}^{\nu}F_{a,c}(u)^{\nu}D_{c}(u)^{\nu}E_{c,b}(u).

Denote the
(i,j)
-entry of
{}^{\nu}D_{a}(u)
,
{}^{\nu}E_{a,b}(u)
, and
{}^{\nu}F_{b,a}(u)
by

{}^{\nu}D_{a;i,j}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}{}^{\nu}D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r},\qquad 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\nu_{a},

{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r},\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a},1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b},

{}^{\nu}F_{b,a;i,j}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}{}^{\nu}F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r},\qquad 1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a},1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{b},

respectively, where
{}^{\nu}D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}
,
{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}
,
{}^{\nu}F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}
are elements of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
.

Define
{}^{\nu}|i|_{a}
for
1\leqslant a\leqslant z
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
by the rule:

{}^{\nu}|i|_{a}:=|\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{a-1}+i|.

We shall often omit the superscript
\nu
and write simply
D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}
,
E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}
,
F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}
. We also use the abbreviation:
E_{a;i,j}^{(r)}:=E_{a,a+1;i,j}^{(r)}
,
F_{a;i,j}^{(r)}:=F_{a+1,a;i,j}^{(r)}
.

The anti-isomorphism
\tau
from (
2.33
) satisfies

\displaystyle\tau(D_{a;i,j}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|i|_{a}|j|_{a}+|j|_{a}}D_{a;i,j}^{(r)},

\displaystyle\tau(E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|i|_{a}|j|_{b}+|j|_{b}}E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)},

\displaystyle\tau(F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|i|_{b}|j|_{a}+|j|_{a}}F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}.

It is known from
[
40
, §3]
that
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
is generated by the elements

\displaystyle\big\{D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a\leqslant z,1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\nu_{a},r>0\big\},

\displaystyle\big\{E_{a;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a<m,1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a},1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a+1},r>s_{a,a+1}(\nu)\big\},

\displaystyle\big\{F_{a;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a<m,1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a+1},1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a},r>s_{a+1,a}(\nu)\big\}

subject to relations recorded explicitly in
[
41
, (5.3)–(5.18)]
(see also
[
40
]
). Moreover, the supermonomials in the elements

\displaystyle\big\{D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a\leqslant z,1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\nu_{a},r>0\big\},

\displaystyle\big\{E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a<b\leqslant z,1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a},1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b},r>s_{a,b}(\nu)\big\},

\displaystyle\big\{F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}\mid 1\leqslant a<b\leqslant z,1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{b},1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a},r>s_{b,a}(\nu)\big\}

taken in any fixed order form a basis of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
. Note that the definition of the higher order root elements
E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}
and
E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}
given here is different from
[
40
]
but the same as in
[
41
]
. The equivalence of the two definitions can be verified by Lemma
A.1
below.

A.2.
Preliminary lemmas

Lemma A.1

(
[
8
, Lem. 2.2]
)
.

For
1\leqslant a<b-1<m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b}
and
r>s_{a,b}(\nu)
, we have

E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}=(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\big[E_{a,b-1;i,g}^{(r-s_{b-1,b}(\nu))},E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
. Similarly, for
1\leqslant a<b-1<m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{b}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a}
and
r>s_{b,a}(\nu)
, we have

F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}=(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\big[F_{b-1;i,g}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)},F_{b-1,a;g,j}^{(r-s_{b-1,b}(\nu))}\big]

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the identities for the
E
’s as that of
F
’s follow by applying the anti-isomorphism
\tau
from (
2.33
). We proceed by backward induction on the length of the admissible shape
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{z})
. The base case
z=m+n
follows by definition (
2.25
). Now suppose
z<m+n
. Pick
1\leqslant p\leqslant m+n
and
x,y>0
such that
\nu_{p}=x+y
, and then let
\mu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{p-1},x,y,\nu_{p+1},\dots,\nu_{z})
, an admissible shape of length strictly less than that of
\nu
. Then a straightforward calculation implies that for each
1\leqslant a<b\leqslant m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b}
that

{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=\begin{cases}{}^{\mu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)&\text{ if }b<p,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)&\text{ if }b=p,j\leqslant x,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a,b+1;i,j-x}(u)&\text{ if }b=p,j>x,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a,b+1;i,j}(u)&\text{ if }a<p,b>p,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a,b+1;i,j}(u)-\sum_{l=1}^{y}{}^{\mu}E_{a;i,l}(u){}^{\mu}E_{a+1,b+1;l,j}(u)&\text{ if }a=p,i\leqslant x,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a+1,b+1;i-x,j}(u)&\text{ if }a=p,i>x,\\
{}^{\mu}E_{a+1,b+1;i,j}(u)&\text{ if }a>p.\end{cases}

For
b>a+1
, we need to prove that

(A.3)

^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}

for each
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
. We shall rewrite both sides of the identity that we need to prove in terms of the
{}^{\mu}E
’s and then use the induction hypothesis

{}^{\mu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\mu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\mu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\mu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{s_{b-1,b}(\mu)+1}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\mu)}

for each
1\leqslant a<b-1\leqslant m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\mu_{a}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\mu_{b}
and
1\leqslant g\leqslant\mu_{b-1}
.

We proceed case by case.

(1) If
b<p
or
b=p,j\leqslant x
, then it is obvious.

(2) Suppose
b=p,j>x
. By induction hypothesis, we have

{}^{\mu}E_{a,b+1;i,j-x}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\mu}|h|_{b}}\big[{}^{\mu}E_{a,b;i,h}(u),{}^{\mu}E_{b;h,j-x}\big]u^{-s_{b,b+1}(\mu)}

for
1\leqslant h\leqslant x
. Since
s_{b,b+1}(\mu)=0
(as
\nu
is admissible) and
{}^{\mu}E_{b;h,j-x}^{(1)}={}^{\nu}D_{b;h,j}^{(1)}
, we find that

(A.4)

^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|h|_{b}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,h}(u),{}^{\nu}D_{b;h,j}^{(1)}\big].

Thus using the relations established in the previous cases and the defining relations of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
, we find that

\displaystyle(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|h|_{b}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,h}(u),{}^{\nu}D_{b;h,j}^{(1)}\big]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|h|_{b}+{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\Big[\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,h}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big],{}^{\nu}D_{b;h,j}^{(1)}\Big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|h|_{b}+{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\Big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),\big[{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,h}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)},{}^{\nu}D_{b;h,j}^{(1)}\big]\Big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
.

(3) Suppose
a<p,b>p
. If
b>p+1
or
b=p+1,g>x
, then it is easy. Now consider the case
b=p+1
and
g\leqslant x
. It is known in case (2) that

(A.5)

^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|x+1|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,x+1}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;x+1,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}.

Using the cases already considered to express
{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}^{(r)}
as a commutator then using the relation
[
41
, (5.13)]
, one has that
[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}^{(r)},{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;x+1,j}^{(s)}]=0
. Applying
[{}^{\nu}D_{b-1;g,x+1}^{(1)},?]
to it and using the relation (
A.4
), we have

(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|x+1|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,x+1}^{(r)},{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;x+1,j}^{(s)}\big]=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}^{(r)},{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s)}\big].

In particular,

(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|x+1|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,x+1}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;x+1,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big].

Combining this with (
A.4
), we get that

{}^{\nu}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)=(-1)^{{}^{\nu}|g|_{b-1}}\big[{}^{\nu}E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),{}^{\nu}E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}

as required.

(4) Suppose
a=p,i\leqslant x
. The LHS of (
A.3
) is equal to

{}^{\mu}E_{a,b+1;i,j}(u)-\sum_{l=1}^{y}{}^{\mu}E_{a;i,l}(u){}^{\mu}E_{a+1,b+1;l,j}(u)

while the RHS equals

(-1)^{{}^{\mu}|g|_{b}}\Big[^{\mu}E_{a,b;i,g}(u)-\sum_{l=1}^{y}{}^{\mu}E_{a;i,l}(u){}^{\mu}E_{a+1,b;l,g}(u),{}^{\mu}E_{b;g,j}^{(s_{b,b+1}(\mu)+1)}\Big]u^{-s_{b,b+1}(\mu)}.

Note that
{}^{\mu}E_{a;i,l}(u)
and
{}^{\mu}E_{b;g,j}^{(s_{b,b+1}(\mu)+1)}
supercommute. The equality (
A.3
) follows from the induction hypothesis.

(5) If
a=p,i>x
or
a>p
, then it is also easy.∎

Introduce another family of elements of
{\mathrm{Y}_{m|n}(\sigma)}
. Recall that
{}^{\nu}E_{a,a}(u)
and
{}^{\nu}F_{a,a}(u)
are both the identity. Define

(A.6)

{}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,b}(u)

\displaystyle:={}^{\nu}E_{a,b}(u)-\sum_{c=a}^{b-1}{}^{\nu}E_{a,c}(u){}^{\nu}E_{c,b}^{(s_{c,b}(\nu)+1)}u^{-s_{c,b}(\nu)-1},

(A.7)

{}^{\nu}\overline{F}_{b,a}(u)

\displaystyle:={}^{\nu}F_{b,a}(u)-\sum_{c=a}^{b-1}{}^{\nu}F_{b,c}^{(s_{b,c}(\nu)+1)}{}^{\nu}F_{c,a}(u)u^{-s_{b,c}(\nu)-1},

for
1\leqslant a<b\leqslant z
. By convention, we have

{}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,a+1}(u)={}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,a+1}(u),\qquad^{\nu}\overline{F}_{a+1,a}(u)={}^{\nu}F_{a+1,a}(u).

As usual, the
ij
-entry of
{}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,b}(u)
and
{}^{\nu}\overline{F}_{b,a}(u)
are denoted by

{}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}(u)=\sum_{r>s_{a,b}(\nu)}^{\nu}\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}u^{-r},\quad^{\nu}\overline{F}_{b,a;j,i}(u)=\sum_{r>s_{b,a}(\nu)}^{\nu}\overline{F}_{b,a;j,i}^{(r)}u^{-r},

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b}
. Again we shall drop the superscript
\nu
.

Lemma A.2

(
[
8
, Lem. 2.3]
)
.

For
1\leqslant a<b-1<m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b}
and
r>s_{a,b}(\nu)+1
, we have

\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}=(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}[E_{a,b-1;i,g}^{(r-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)-1)},E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}]

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
. Similarly, for
1\leqslant a<b-1<m+n
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{b}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{a}
and
r>s_{b,a}(\nu)
, we have

\overline{F}_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}=(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}[F_{b-1;i,g}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)},F_{b-1,a;g,j}^{(r-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)-1)}]

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{b-1}
.

Proof.

Again it suffices to prove the identities for the
E
’s and the identities for the
F
’s will follow by applying the anti-isomorphism
\tau
from (
2.34
). It is equivalent to prove

\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}(u)=\big[E_{a,b;i,g}(u),E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}\big]u^{-(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)-1}.

We prove it by induction on
b\geqslant a+2
. For the base case
b=a+2
, we have by
[
41
, (5.11)]
that

\displaystyle\big[E_{a,b;i,g}(u),E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}\big]-\big[E_{a,b;i,g}(u),E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u

\displaystyle=\big[E_{a,b;i,g}^{(s_{a,b-1}(\nu)+1)},E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}-(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{b-1}}E_{a,b-1;i,h}(u)E_{b-1;h,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}.

Multiplying by
u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)-1}
and applying Lemma
A.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big[E_{a,b;i,g}(u),

\displaystyle\,E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}\big]u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)-1}=(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}E_{a,b;i,j}(u)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}E_{a,b;i,j}^{(s_{a,b}(\nu)+1)}u^{-s_{a,b}(\nu)-1}-(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{b-1}}E_{a,b-1;i,h}(u)E_{b-1;h,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}.

It follows from (
A.6
) that the RHS is exactly
(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}(u)
.

Now suppose that
b>a+2
. Using Lemma
A.1
, the relations
[
41
, (5.11), (5.13)]
, and the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle[E_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}]u^{-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}}\big[[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),E_{b-2;i,g}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+1)}],E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}\big]u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}}\big[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),[E_{b-2;i,g}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+1)},E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+2)}]\big]u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}}\big[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),[E_{b-2;i,g}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+2)},E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}]\big]u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}+|g|_{b-1}}\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{b-1}}\big[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),E_{b-2;1,h}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+1)}E_{b-1;h,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}}\big[[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),E_{b-2;i,g}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+2)}],E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|1|_{b-2}+|g|_{b-1}}\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{b-1}}\big[E_{a,b-2;i,1}(u),E_{b-2;1,h}^{(s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)+1)}\big]E_{b-1;h,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}u^{-s_{b-2,b-1}(\nu)-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\big[\overline{E}_{a,b-1;i,g}(u),E_{b-1;g,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}\big]-(-1)^{|g|_{b-1}}\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{b-1}}E_{a,b-1;i,h}(u)E_{b-1;h,j}^{(s_{b-1,b}(\nu)+1)}u^{-1}.

Now multiplying both sides by
u^{-s_{b-1,b}(\nu)}
and using (
A.6
) together with Lemma
A.1
completes the proof.
∎

A.3.
Finishing the proof

Now we are in the position to prove that
T_{i,j}^{(r)}
in
W(\pi)
is zero whenever
r>s_{i,j}+p_{\min(i,j)}
.

Recall the fixed choice of
k
from (
3.1
) and other notations from Section
3
. Given
k\leqslant\theta\leqslant\ell
, let
\pi_{\theta}
denote the pyramid
(q_{1},\dots,q_{\theta})
of level
\theta
, i.e., the first
\theta
columns of
\pi
. Let
\sigma_{\theta}
be the shift matrix for
\pi_{\theta}
defined by (
3.2
) with the chosen
k
. Denote by
\mathfrak{g}_{\theta}
the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{m_{1}+\dots+m_{\theta}|n_{1}+\dots+n_{\theta}}
and regard it as the subsuperalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
via the canonical embedding. Let
\mathtt{I}_{\theta}:\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\theta})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
be the induced embedding for the universal enveloping superalgebras. We consider elements both of
W(\pi)\subset\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
and of
W(\pi_{\theta})\subset\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\theta})\subset\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g})
. To avoid the confusion, we proceed the latter by the embedding
\mathtt{I}_{\theta}
. For example, the notation
\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)})
in the lemma below means the image of the elements
\overline{E}_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}
of
W(\pi_{\ell-1})
under the embedding
\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}
. We always work relative to the minimal admissible shape
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{z})
for
\sigma
.

Lemma A.3
.

Assume that
q_{1}\geqslant q_{\ell}
and
k<\ell
. Then for all meaningful
a,b,i,j
and
r
, we have

\displaystyle D_{a;i,j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(D_{a;i,j}^{(r)})+\delta_{a,z}\Big(\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{z}}(-1)^{|h|_{z}}\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(D_{z;i,h}^{(r-1)})e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+h,q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}

(A.8)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt-\big[\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(D_{z;i,j}^{(r-1)}),e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j-q_{\ell},q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}\big]\Big),

\displaystyle E_{a,z;i,j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(\overline{E}_{a,z;i,j}^{(r)})+\Big(\sum_{h=1}^{\nu_{z}}(-1)^{|h|_{z}}\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(E_{a,z;i,h}^{(r-1)})e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+h,q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}

(A.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt-\big[\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(E_{a,z;i,j}^{(r-1)}),e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j-q_{\ell},q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}\big]\Big),

(A.10)

\displaystyle E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(E_{a,b;i,j}^{(r)}),\hskip 230.46748pt\text{ for }b<z,

(A.11)

\displaystyle F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(F_{b,a;i,j}^{(r)}).

Proof.

The equations (
A.8
), (
A.9
) for
a=z-1
, and (
A.10
)–(
A.11
) for the special case
b=a+1
follow from
[
41
, Lem. 10.2]
. For the cases
b>a+1
, (
A.10
)–(
A.11
) are proved with the help of Lemma
A.1
by a simple induction on
b-a
.

Then we consider the difficult (
A.9
). Using
[
41
, Thm. 9.2 & (9.5)]
and Lemma
A.1
, it is not hard to see that

\displaystyle\big[\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(E_{a,z-1;i,g}^{(r)}),e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j-q_{\ell},q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}\big]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\big[\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(E_{a,z-1;i,g}^{(r)}),e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+h,q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell-1}+j}\big]=0,

for any
1\leqslant g\leqslant\nu_{z-1}
. Thus, (
A.9
) follows from the above observation and Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Assume that
q_{1}\geqslant q_{\ell}
and
k<\ell
. We have

(1)

for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant m+n-q_{\ell}
and
r>0
we have
T_{i,j}^{(r)}=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{i,j}^{(r)})
;

(2)

for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m+n
,
j>m+n-q_{\ell}
and
r>0
we have

\displaystyle T_{i,j}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{i,j}^{(r)})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant h\leqslant m+n-q_{\ell}\\
s_{h,j}\leqslant r\end{subarray}}\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{i,h}^{(r-s_{h,j})})\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{h,j}^{(s_{h,j})})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49411pt+\sum_{m+n-q_{\ell}<h\leqslant m+n}(-1)^{|h|}\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{i,h}^{(r-1)})e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell}+h-m-n,q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell}+j-m-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 118.07875pt-\big[\mathtt{I}_{\ell-1}(T_{i,j}^{(r-1)}),e_{q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell}+j-m-n,q_{1}+\dots+q_{\ell}+j-m-n}\big].

Proof.

For
1\leqslant a,b\leqslant z
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\nu_{a}
, and
1\leqslant j\leqslant\nu_{b}
, we have by definition that

T_{\nu_{1}+\dots+\nu_{a-1}+i,\nu_{1}+\dots+\nu_{b-1}+j}(u)=\sum_{c=1}^{\min(a,b)}\sum_{s,t=1}^{\nu_{c}}F_{a,c;i,s}(u)D_{c;s,t}(u)E_{c,b;t,j}(u).

Apply Lemma
A.3
to rewrite the RHS along with (
A.6
)–(
A.7
). After a straightforward calculation
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

We prove it by induction on
\ell
. The base case
\ell=1
is verified directly by definition. Using the anti-automorphism
\tau
if necessary, we can assume that
q_{1}\geqslant q_{\ell}
and hence fix a choice of
k
such that
k<\ell
. Note that if
i,h\leqslant j
, then

(s_{i,j}+p_{i})-s_{h,j}=s_{i,h}+p_{\min(i,h)}.

Thus, the induction hypothesis implies that all the terms in the RHS of Lemma
A.4
are zero if
r>s_{i,j}+p_{\min(i,j)}
, completing the proof.
∎
